(e_{1},e_{2})
is responsible for
\operatorname{cong}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}}(e_{1})\cdot\operatorname{cong}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}}(e_{2})\cdot\Delta^{4}
crossings in
\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{r}\Upsilon^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}
. If
\operatorname{cong}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}}(e_{1})\leq\operatorname{cong}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}}(e_{2})
, then we make edge
e_{2}
responsible for all these crossings and charge it
(\operatorname{cong}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}}(e_{2}))^{2}\cdot\Delta^{4}\geq\operatorname{cong}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}}(e_{1})\cdot\operatorname{cong}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}}(e_{2})\cdot\Delta^{4}
for them, and otherwise, we make edge
e_{1}
responsible for these crossings, and charge it
(\operatorname{cong}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}}(e_{1}))^{2}\cdot\Delta^{4}\geq\operatorname{cong}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}}(e_{1})\cdot\operatorname{cong}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}}(e_{2})\cdot\Delta^{4}
for them.

If
(e_{1},e_{2})
is a crossing in
\varphi
where exactly one of the two edges
e_{1},e_{2}
lies in some cluster
C_{i}
and the other edge lies in
E^{\prime\prime}
, then the analysis is similar except that this crossing only contributes to
\Upsilon^{\prime}_{i}
and is charged to the corresponding edge. If both
e_{1},e_{2}
lie in the same cluster
C_{i}
, then crossing
(e_{1},e_{2})
does not contribute to
\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{r}\Upsilon^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}
.
Recall that for every cluster
C_{i}\in{\mathcal{C}}
, for every edge
e\in E(C_{i}^{\prime})
, we have defined weight
w(e)
, which is at least the number of crossings in the drawing
\varphi
of
G
in which edge
e
participates.
To summarize, from the above discussion, we get that:

\sum_{i=1}^{r}\Upsilon^{\prime}_{i}\leq O\left(\sum_{i=1}^{r}\sum_{e\in C^{\prime}_{i}}\Delta^{4}\cdot w(e)\left(\operatorname{cong}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}}(e)\right)^{2}\right).

Altogether, we then get that:

\mathsf{cr}(\varphi^{\prime})-\mathsf{cr}(\varphi)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{r}O\left(\Delta^{6}\cdot\sum_{e\in C^{\prime}_{i}}w(e)\left(\operatorname{cong}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}}(e)\right)^{2}\right).

Final Accounting.

Recall that we have denoted, for every cluster
C_{i}\in{\mathcal{C}}
, by

\mathsf{IRG}_{i}\subseteq E(J_{i})
the set of all edges
e\in E(J_{i})
, such that either
e
is irregular with respect to the drawings
\psi_{J_{i}}
and
\varphi_{J_{i}}
of
J_{i}
, or at least one endpoint of
e
is irregular with respect to these drawings. We also denote by
x_{i}
the total number of crossings in the drawing
\varphi
of
G
in which the edges of
C^{\prime}_{i}
participate. It is then easy to verify that for every cluster
C_{i}\in{\mathcal{C}}
,
\sum_{e\in E(C^{\prime}_{i})}w(e)\leq x_{i}+|\mathsf{IRG}_{i}|
.

Consider now some cluster
C_{i}\in{\mathcal{C}}
, and assume first that
C_{i}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
. From Equation
3
:

\begin{split}\sum_{e\in E(C_{i})}w(e)\cdot(\operatorname{cong}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}}(e))^{2}&\leq O(\mu^{2}\Delta^{2})\cdot\sum_{e\in E(C_{i})}w(e)\\
&\leq O(\mu^{2}\Delta^{2})(x_{i}+|\mathsf{IRG}_{i}|)\\
&\leq O(\mu^{3}\Delta^{3}(x_{i}+1))\\
&\leq O(\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n)(x_{i}+1)).\end{split}

(We have used the fact that, if
C_{i}
is a type-1 cluster then
|\mathsf{IRG}_{i}|\leq O(\mu\Delta)
, and that
\mu=O(\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n)
.)

Assume now that
C_{i}
is a type-2 cluster. From Equation
5
, we get that:

\sum_{e\in E(C^{\prime}_{i})}w(e)\cdot(\operatorname{cong}_{{\mathcal{Q}}}(e))^{2}\leq O\left(\frac{\log n}{\alpha^{4}}\cdot\left(\sum_{e\in E(C_{i})}w^{\prime}(e)+1\right)\right).

Let
\mathsf{IRG}_{i}^{\prime}\subseteq E(C_{i}^{\prime})
denote the set of all edges
e\in E(C_{i}^{\prime})
, such that either
e
is an irregular edge with respect to the drawing
\psi_{C^{\prime}_{i}}
of
C^{\prime}_{i}
, and the drawing
\varphi_{C^{\prime}_{i}}
of
C^{\prime}_{i}
induced by
\varphi
, or at least one endpoint of
e
is irregular with respect to these drawings. Recall that
\sum_{e\in E(C_{i}^{\prime})}w^{\prime}(e)=O(x_{i}+|\mathsf{IRG}_{i}^{\prime}|)
. Therefore, we get that:

\sum_{e\in E(C^{\prime}_{i})}w(e)\cdot(\operatorname{cong}_{{\mathcal{Q}}}(e))^{2}\leq O\left(\frac{\log n}{\alpha^{4}}\cdot(x_{i}+|\mathsf{IRG}^{\prime}_{i}|+1)\right)\leq O\left(\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n)\cdot(x_{i}+|\mathsf{IRG}^{\prime}_{i}|+1)\right),

since
\alpha=\Theta(1/\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n))
.
Altogether, the number of crossings in the new drawing
\varphi^{\prime}
of
\varphi
can now be bounded as:

\begin{split}\mathsf{cr}(\varphi^{\prime})&\leq\mathsf{cr}(\varphi)+O\left(\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n)\right)\cdot\left(\sum_{i=1}^{r}x_{i}+\sum_{C_{i}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{2}}|\mathsf{IRG}_{i}^{\prime}|+|{\mathcal{C}}|\right)\\
&\leq O\left(\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n)(\mathsf{cr}(\varphi)+|E^{\prime\prime}|)\right)+O\left(\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n)\right)\cdot\sum_{C_{i}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{2}}|\mathsf{IRG}_{i}^{\prime}|.\end{split}

The next claim will then finish the proof of Theorem
3.4
.

Claim 7.7
.

\sum_{C_{i}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{2}}|\mathsf{IRG}_{i}^{\prime}|\leq O\left(\Delta^{2}(|E^{\prime\prime}|+\mathsf{cr}(\varphi))\right)
.

Proof.

Consider some cluster
C_{i}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
. Recall that set
\mathsf{IRG}_{i}^{\prime}\subseteq E(C_{i}^{\prime})
contains all edges
e\in E(C_{i}^{\prime})
, such that either
e
is an irregular edge with respect to the drawing
\psi_{C^{\prime}_{i}}
of
C^{\prime}_{i}
, and the drawing
\varphi_{C^{\prime}_{i}}
of
C^{\prime}_{i}
induced by
\varphi
, or at least one endpoint of
e
is irregular with respect to these drawings. In other words,
|\mathsf{IRG}_{i}^{\prime}|\leq\Delta\cdot|\mathsf{IRG}_{V}(\varphi_{C^{\prime}_{i}},\psi_{C^{\prime}_{i}})|+|\mathsf{IRG}_{E}(\varphi_{C^{\prime}_{i}},\psi_{C^{\prime}_{i}})|
. Lemma
7.2
guarantees that:

|\mathsf{IRG}_{V}(\varphi_{C^{\prime}_{i}},\psi_{C^{\prime}_{i}})\setminus S_{2}(C^{\prime}_{i})|+|\mathsf{IRG}_{E}(\varphi_{C^{\prime}_{i}},\psi_{C^{\prime}_{i}})\setminus E_{2}(C^{\prime}_{i})|\leq O(\mathsf{cr}(\varphi_{C^{\prime}_{i}}))\leq O(x_{i}),

where
S_{2}(C^{\prime}_{i})
is the set of all vertices that participate in
2
-separators in
C^{\prime}_{i}
, and
E_{2}(C^{\prime}_{i})
is the set of all edges of
C^{\prime}_{i}
that have both endpoints in
S_{2}(C^{\prime}_{i})
. Unfortunately, the definition of type-2 acceptable clusters does not provide any bound on the cardinality of the set
S_{2}(C^{\prime}_{i})
. It does, however, ensure that
|S_{2}(C_{i})|\leq O(\Delta|\Gamma(C_{i})|)
, where
C_{i}
is the original cluster, that may contain artificial edges. Unfortunately, the original drawing
\varphi
of
G
does not include the drawings of the artificial edges. However, using the embeddings of these edges, we can easily transform drawing
\varphi
of
G
into a drawing
\tilde{\varphi}
of
\bigcup_{C_{i}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{2}}C_{i}
, without increasing the number of crossings. Applying Lemma
7.2
to the resulting drawings of graphs
C_{i}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
will then finish the proof. We now turn to provide a more detailed proof.

Consider the original drawing
\varphi
of graph
G
. We transform it into a drawing
\tilde{\varphi}
of
\bigcup_{C_{i}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{2}}C_{i}
, as follows. Recall that the decomposition
{\mathcal{D}}
of
G
into acceptable clusters contains an embedding
{\mathcal{P}}=\left\{P(e)\mid e\in A\right\}
of all artificial edges via paths that are internally disjoint. Moreover, for every edge
e=(x,y)\in A
, there is a type-1 cluster
C(e)\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
, such that
P(e)\setminus\left\{x,y\right\}
is contained in
C(e)
, and the clusters
C(e)
are distinct for all edges
e\in A
. We delete from
\varphi
all vertices and edges except those participating in graphs
C^{\prime}_{i}
for
C_{i}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
, and in paths in
{\mathcal{P}}
. By suppressing all inner vertices on the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}
, we obtain a drawing
\tilde{\varphi}
of
\bigcup_{C_{i}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{2}}C_{i}
, that contains at most
\mathsf{cr}(\varphi)
crossings. Consider now some cluster
C_{i}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
. Let
\tilde{\varphi}_{i}
be the drawing of
C_{i}
that is induced by
\tilde{\varphi}
. Observe that, if a vertex
v\in V(C_{i})
is irregular with respect to

\varphi_{C^{\prime}_{i}}
,
\psi_{C^{\prime}_{i}}
, then it must be irregular with respect to
\tilde{\varphi}_{i}
and
\psi^{\prime}_{C_{i}}
. Similarly, if an edge
e\in E(C^{\prime}_{i})
is irregular with respect to

\varphi_{C^{\prime}_{i}}
,
\psi_{C^{\prime}_{i}}
, then it must be irregular with respect to
\tilde{\varphi}_{i}
and
\psi^{\prime}_{C_{i}}
. Therefore, if we denote by
\mathsf{IRG}_{i}^{\prime\prime}
the set of all edges
e\in C_{i}
, such that either
e
is irregular with respect to
\tilde{\varphi}_{i}
and
\psi^{\prime}_{C_{i}}
, or at least one endpoint of
e
is irregular with respect to these two drawings, then
|\mathsf{IRG}_{i}^{\prime\prime}|\geq|\mathsf{IRG}_{i}^{\prime}|
. Let
x^{\prime}_{i}
be the total number of crossings in
\tilde{\varphi}_{i}
. Let
E_{2}^{i}\subseteq E(C_{i})
be the set of all edges that are incident to vertices of
S_{2}(C_{i})
(vertices that participate in
2
-separators in
C_{i}
). Then, from Lemma
7.2
:

|\mathsf{IRG}_{i}^{\prime\prime}\setminus E_{2}^{i}|\leq O(\Delta\cdot x^{\prime}_{i}).

Moreover, from the definition of type-2 acceptable clusters,
|S_{2}(C_{i})|\leq O(\Delta|\Gamma(C_{i})|)
, and so
|E_{2}^{i}|\leq O(\Delta^{2}|\Gamma(C_{i})|)
. Overall, we conclude that:

|\mathsf{IRG}_{i}^{\prime}|\leq|\mathsf{IRG}_{i}^{\prime\prime}|\leq O(\Delta^{2}(x^{\prime}_{i}+|\Gamma(C_{i})|)).

Summing up over all clusters
C_{i}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
, we get that:

\begin{split}\sum_{C_{i}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{2}}|\mathsf{IRG}_{i}^{\prime}|&\leq O(\Delta^{2})\sum_{C_{i}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{2}}x^{\prime}_{i}+O(\Delta^{2})\sum_{C_{i}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{2}}|\Gamma(C_{i})|\\
&\leq O\left(\Delta^{2}\cdot(\mathsf{cr}(\tilde{\varphi})+|E^{\prime\prime}|)\right)\\
&\leq O\left(\Delta^{2}\cdot(\mathsf{cr}(\varphi)+|E^{\prime\prime}|)\right).\end{split}

8
Handling Non 3-Connected Graphs

So far we have provided the proofs of Theorem
1.1
and Theorem
1.3
for the special case where the input graph
G
is
3
-connected.
In this section we extend the proofs to arbitrary graphs, and also provide the proof of Theorem
3.5
. We start by extending the proof of Theorem
1.1
to arbitrary graphs.

It is sufficient to prove Theorem
1.1
for the special case where the input graph
G
is
2
-connected. Indeed, given any graph
G
, let
{\mathcal{Z}}(G)
be the set of all super-blocks of
G
(maximal
2
-connected components). Given a planarizing set
E^{\prime}
of edges for graph
G
, for each graph
Z\in{\mathcal{Z}}(G)
, we let
E^{\prime}(Z)=E^{\prime}\cap E(G)
. We then apply the algorithm from Theorem
1.1
to each graph
Z\in{\mathcal{Z}}
, together with the planarizing set
E^{\prime}(Z)
of its edges separately, obtaining a new planarizing edge set
E^{\prime\prime}(Z)
with
E^{\prime}(Z)\subseteq E^{\prime\prime}(Z)
. Moreover, from Theorem
1.1
, we are guaranteed that:

\sum_{Z\in{\mathcal{Z}}(G)}|E^{\prime\prime}(Z)|\leq\sum_{Z\in{\mathcal{Z}}(G)}O\left((|E^{\prime}(Z)|+\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(Z))\cdot\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n)\right)\leq O\left((|E^{\prime}|+\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(G))\cdot\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n)\right).

We are also guaranteed that, for each graph
Z\in{\mathcal{Z}}
, there is a drawing
\varphi(Z)
of
Z
, such that the number of crossings in
\varphi(Z)
is bounded by
O\left((|E^{\prime}(Z)|+\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(Z))\cdot\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n)\right)
, and the edges of
G\setminus E^{\prime\prime}(Z)
do not participate in any crossings in
\varphi(Z)
. We set
E^{\prime\prime}=\bigcup_{Z\in{\mathcal{Z}}}E^{\prime\prime}(Z)
, so that
|E^{\prime\prime}|\leq O\left((|E^{\prime}|+\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(G))\cdot\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n)\right)
. It is also easy to combine the drawings
\left\{\varphi(Z)\right\}_{Z\in{\mathcal{Z}}}
into a drawing
\varphi
of
G
, without introducing any new crossings, so that the total number of crossings in
\varphi
is at most:

\sum_{Z\in{\mathcal{Z}}(G)}O\left((|E^{\prime}(Z)|+\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(Z))\cdot\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n)\right)\leq O\left((|E^{\prime}|+\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(G))\cdot\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n)\right),

and the edges of
E^{\prime\prime}
do not participate in crossings in
\varphi
.

Therefore, from now on we assume that the input graph
G
is
2
-connected. We let
{\mathcal{B}}={\mathcal{B}}(G)
be the block decomposition of
G
given by Theorem
4.1
. Let
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}=\left\{\tilde{B}\mid B\in{\mathcal{B}}\right\}
, and let
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{*}\subseteq\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
contain all graphs
\tilde{B}
that are not isomorphic to
K_{3}
. We use the algorithm from Lemma
4.4
to compute, for each graph
\tilde{B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{*}
, a collection
{\mathcal{P}}_{\tilde{B}}=\left\{P_{\tilde{B}}(e)\mid e\in A_{\tilde{B}}\right\}
of paths in
G
, such that, for each fake edge
e=(u,v)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\tilde{B}}
, path
P_{\tilde{B}}(e)
connects
u
to
v
in
G
and it is internally disjoint from
\tilde{B}
. Recall that the lemma guarantees that for every graph
\tilde{B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
, all paths in
{\mathcal{P}}_{\tilde{B}}
are mutually internally disjoint, and moreover, if we denote
{\mathcal{P}}=\bigcup_{\tilde{B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{*}(G)}\left({\mathcal{P}}_{\tilde{B}}\setminus\left\{P_{\tilde{B}}(e^{*}_{\tilde{B}})\right\}\right)
, then every edge of
G
participates in at most
6
paths in
{\mathcal{P}}
. For simplicity of notation, for each graph
\tilde{B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
, we refer to the fake parent-edge
e^{*}_{\tilde{B}}
as the
bad fake edge
of
\tilde{B}
; all other fake edges of
\tilde{B}
are called good fake edges.

Assume that we are given a planarizing set
E^{\prime}
of edges for the input graph
G
. The next lemma allows us to compute, for each graph
\tilde{B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
, a planarizing set
E^{\prime}(\tilde{B})
of edges for
\tilde{B}
, so that
\sum_{B\in{\mathcal{B}}}|E^{\prime}(\tilde{B})|
is sufficiently small.

Lemma 8.1
.

There is an efficient algorithm, that, given a planarizing set
E^{\prime}
of edges for graph
G
, computes, for each graph
\tilde{B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
, a planarizing edge set
E^{\prime}(\tilde{B})
for
\tilde{B}
, such that
\sum_{\tilde{B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}}|E^{\prime}(\tilde{B})|\leq O(|E^{\prime}|+\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(G))
.

Proof.

Consider a graph
\tilde{B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
. In order to construct the planarizing set
E^{\prime}(\tilde{B})
of edges, we start by adding every real edge of
\tilde{B}
that lies in
E^{\prime}
to set
E^{\prime}(\tilde{B})
. Additionally, for every fake edge
e\in\tilde{B}
that is not a bad fake edge, if the path
P_{\tilde{B}}(e)\in{\mathcal{P}}_{\tilde{B}}
contains an edge of
E^{\prime}
, then we add
e
to
E^{\prime}(\tilde{B})
. Since every edge of
G
may belong to at most
6
paths in
{\mathcal{P}}
, this ensures that
\sum_{\tilde{B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}}|E^{\prime}(\tilde{B})|\leq O(|E^{\prime}|)
. If
\tilde{B}\setminus E^{\prime}(\tilde{B})
is a planar graph, then we say that
B
is a
good block
, and otherwise we say that it is a bad block. We need the following claim.

Claim 8.2
.

If
B
is a bad block, then graph
\tilde{B}\setminus(E^{\prime}(\tilde{B})\cup e^{*}_{\tilde{B}})
is planar.

Proof.

Notice that every real edge of
\tilde{B}\setminus(E^{\prime}(\tilde{B})\cup e^{*}_{\tilde{B}})
belongs to
G\setminus E^{\prime}
, and for every fake edge
e\in E(\tilde{B})\setminus(E^{\prime}(\tilde{B})\cup e^{*}_{\tilde{B}})
, the corresponding path
P_{\tilde{B}}(e)
is contained in
G\setminus E^{\prime}
. Recall that graph
G\setminus E^{\prime}
is planar. It is now immediate to verify that a planar drawing of
G\setminus E^{\prime}
defines a planar drawing of
\tilde{B}\setminus(E^{\prime}(\tilde{B})\cup e^{*}_{\tilde{B}})
.

The next claim will then finish the proof of Lemma
8.1
.

Claim 8.3
.

The total number of bad blocks in
{\mathcal{B}}
is bounded by
O(\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(G))
.

If
B
is a bad block, then we add the bad fake edge
e^{*}_{\tilde{B}}
to set
E^{\prime}(\tilde{B})
. From Claim
8.2
, set
E^{\prime}(\tilde{B})
of edges is a planarizing set for graph
\tilde{B}
. Moreover, from the above analysis,
\sum_{B\in{\mathcal{B}}}|E^{\prime}(\tilde{B})|\leq O(|E^{\prime}|+\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(G))
, as required.
Therefore, in order to complete the proof of Lemma
8.1
, it is enough to prove Claim
8.3
.

Proof of Claim
8.3
.

Consider the optimal drawing
\varphi^{*}
of graph
G
. We erase from this drawing all edges and vertices, except for the vertices and the real edges of
\tilde{B}
, and all vertices and edges participating in the paths
{\mathcal{P}}_{\tilde{B}}
. The resulting drawing can be viewed as a drawing of the graph
\tilde{B}
, after we suppress all inner vertices on the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}_{\tilde{B}}
. Notice that, since
B
is a bad block, there is some pair
e_{1},e_{2}
of edges that crosses in the resulting drawing, and moreover,
e_{1},e_{2}
do not both belong to the path
P_{\tilde{B}}(e^{*}_{\tilde{B}})\in{\mathcal{P}}_{\tilde{B}}
. Therefore, at least one of the two edges
e_{1},e_{2}
, is either a real edge of
\tilde{B}
, or it lies on some path
P_{\tilde{B}}(e)
, where
e
is a good fake edge for
\tilde{B}
. We say that the crossing
(e_{1},e_{2})
of
\varphi^{*}
is
responsible
for the bad block
B
. Since every edge of
G
may participate in at most one graph
\tilde{B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
as a real edge, and since every edge of
G
may belong to at most
6
paths in
{\mathcal{P}}
, we get that every crossing in
\varphi^{*}
may only be responsible for a constant number of bad blocks, and so the total number of bad blocks is bounded by
O(\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(G))
.

We also need the following lemma:

Lemma 8.4
.

\sum_{B\in{\mathcal{B}}}\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(\tilde{B})\leq O(\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(G))
.

Proof.

Let
\varphi^{*}
be the optimal drawing of
G
, so
\mathsf{cr}(\varphi^{*})=\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(G)
. We assume w.l.o.g. that every pair of edges crosses at most once in
\varphi^{*}
, and for every edge, its image does not cross itself in
\varphi^{*}
. We now define, for each graph
\tilde{B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
, a drawing
\psi_{\tilde{B}}
in the plane, using the drawing
\varphi^{*}
. Consider any graph
\tilde{B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
. Note that, if
\tilde{B}
is isomorphic to
K_{3}
, then it has a planar drawing, so we let
\psi_{\tilde{B}}
be that planar drawing. Assume now that
\tilde{B}
is not isomorphic to
K_{3}
, so
\tilde{B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{*}
.

In order to obtain the drawing
\psi_{\tilde{B}}
of
\tilde{B}
, we start from the drawing
\varphi^{*}
of
G
, and delete from it all edges and vertices, except for the vertices and the real edges of
\tilde{B}
, and all vertices and edges participating in the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}_{\tilde{B}}
.
We partition all crossings of the resulting drawing into five sets. Set
\chi_{1}(\tilde{B})
contains all crossings
(e_{1},e_{2})
, where both
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
are real edges of
\tilde{B}
. Set
\chi_{2}(\tilde{B})
contains all crossings
(e_{1},e_{2})
, where
e_{1}
is a real edge of
\tilde{B}
, and
e_{2}
lies on some path
P_{\tilde{B}}(e)
, where
e
is a fake edge of
\tilde{B}
. Set
\chi_{3}(\tilde{B})
contains all crossings
(e_{1},e_{2})
, where
e_{1}\in P_{\tilde{B}}(e)
,
e_{2}\in P_{\tilde{B}}(e^{\prime})
; both
e
and
e^{\prime}
are fake edges of
\tilde{B}
(where possibly
e=e^{\prime}
); and neither of these edges is the parent-edge
e^{*}_{\tilde{B}}
. Set
\chi_{4}(\tilde{B})
contains all crossings
(e_{1},e_{2})
, where
e_{1}\in P_{\tilde{B}}(e)
,
e_{2}\in P_{\tilde{B}}(e^{\prime})
, such that
e,e^{\prime}
are both fake edges of
\tilde{B}
, and exactly one of these edges is the parent-edge
e^{*}_{\tilde{B}}
. Lastly, set
\chi_{5}(\tilde{B})
contains all crossings
(e_{1},e_{2})
, where both
e_{1},e_{2}\in P_{\tilde{B}}(e^{*}_{\tilde{B}})
.
We obtain the final drawing
\psi_{\tilde{B}}
of
\tilde{B}
from the current drawing, by suppressing all inner vertices on the paths of
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tilde{B}}
. Additionally, if the image of the edge
e^{*}_{\tilde{B}}
crosses itself, then we remove all loops to ensure that this does not happen. Clearly,
\mathsf{cr}(\psi_{\tilde{B}})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{4}|\chi_{i}(\tilde{B})|
.

We denote, for all
1\leq i\leq 4
,
\chi_{i}=\bigcup_{\tilde{B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{*}}\chi_{i}(\tilde{B})
(we view
\chi_{i}
as a multiset, so a crossing
(e_{1},e_{2})
that belongs to several sets
\chi_{i}(\tilde{B})
is added several times to
\chi_{i}
). It is now enough to show that
\sum_{i=1}^{4}|\chi_{i}|\leq O(\mathsf{cr}(\varphi^{*}))
.

Consider some crossing
(e_{1},e_{2})
in
\varphi^{*}
. Observe that this crossing may lie in set
\chi_{1}(\tilde{B})
for a graph
\tilde{B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{*}
only if both
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
are real edges of
\tilde{B}
. Since each edge of
G
may belong to at most one graph in
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
as a real edge, crossing
(e_{1},e_{2})
appears at most once in
\chi_{1}
. Therefore,
|\chi_{1}|\leq\mathsf{cr}(\varphi^{*})
. Notice that crossing
(e_{1},e_{2})
may lie in set
\chi_{2}(\tilde{B})
for a graph
\tilde{B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{*}
only if either
e_{1}
or
e_{2}
are real edges of
\tilde{B}
. Therefore, using the same reasoning as before, crossing
(e_{1},e_{2})
may appear at most twice in
\chi_{2}
, and so
|\chi_{2}|\leq 2\mathsf{cr}(\varphi^{*})
. Assume now that
(e_{1},e_{2})\in\chi_{3}(\tilde{B})
, for some graph
\tilde{B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{*}
.
Then there are fake edges
e,e^{\prime}
in
\tilde{B}
, neither of which is the parent-edge
e^{*}_{\tilde{B}}
, such that
e_{1}\in P_{\tilde{B}}(e)
and
e_{2}\in P_{\tilde{B}}(e^{\prime})
(where it is possible that
e=e^{\prime}
). Since each edge of
G
may belong to at most
6
paths in
{\mathcal{P}}
, we get that crossing
(e_{1},e_{2})
may appear at most
6
times in
\chi_{3}
, so
|\chi_{3}|\leq 6\mathsf{cr}(\varphi^{*})
. Lastly, assume that
(e_{1},e_{2})\in\chi_{4}(\tilde{B})
, for some graph
\tilde{B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{*}
.
Then there are fake edges
e,e^{\prime}
in
\tilde{B}
, exactly one of which is the parent-edge
e^{*}_{\tilde{B}}
, such that
e_{1}\in P_{\tilde{B}}(e)
and
e_{2}\in P_{\tilde{B}}(e^{\prime})
. Since each edge of
G
may belong to at most
6
paths in
{\mathcal{P}}
, we get that crossing
(e_{1},e_{2})
may appear at most
12
times in
\chi_{4}
, so
|\chi_{4}|\leq 12\mathsf{cr}(\varphi^{*})
.

Overall, we get that
\sum_{\tilde{B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}}\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(\tilde{B})\leq\sum_{\tilde{B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}}\mathsf{cr}(\psi_{\tilde{B}})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{4}|\chi_{i}|\leq O(\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(G))
.

We are now ready to complete the proof of Theorem
1.1
.
Assume that we are given a planarizing set
E^{\prime}
of edges for graph
G
. Using the algorithm from Lemma
8.1
, we compute, for each graph
\tilde{B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
, a planarizing edge set
E^{\prime}(\tilde{B})
, such that
\sum_{B\in{\mathcal{B}}}|E^{\prime}(\tilde{B})|\leq O(|E^{\prime}|+\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(G))
. Then we use our algorithm for Theorem
1.1
in
3
-connected graphs to compute, for each graph
\tilde{B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
, another planarizing set
E^{\prime\prime}(\tilde{B})
with
E^{\prime}(\tilde{B})\subseteq E^{\prime\prime}(\tilde{B})
, such that
|E^{\prime\prime}(\tilde{B})|\leq O\left((|E^{\prime}(\tilde{B})|+\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(\tilde{B}))\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n)\right)
, and there is a drawing
\varphi_{\tilde{B}}
of graph
\tilde{B}
with at most
O\left((|E^{\prime}(\tilde{B})|+\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(\tilde{B}))\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n)\right)
crossings, such that the edges of
E^{\prime\prime}(\tilde{B})
do not participate in the crossings of
\varphi_{\tilde{B}}
. From Lemma
8.4
, we get that:

\sum_{B\in{\mathcal{B}}}|E^{\prime\prime}(\tilde{B})|\leq O\left((|E^{\prime}|+\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(G))\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n)\right),

and similarly:

\sum_{B\in{\mathcal{B}}}\mathsf{cr}(\varphi_{\tilde{B}})\leq O\left((|E^{\prime}|+\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(G))\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n)\right).

Next, we define the final set
E^{\prime\prime}
of edges for the graph
G
. We start with
E^{\prime\prime}=\emptyset
. For every graph
\tilde{B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
, for every edge
e=(u,v)\in E^{\prime\prime}(\tilde{B})
, if
e
is a real edge, then we add
e
, and every edge of
G
that is incident to either
u
or
v
to
E^{\prime\prime}
. Otherwise,
e
is a fake edge, and then we add to
E^{\prime\prime}
every edge of
G
that is incident to either
u
or
v
. This completes the definition of the set
E^{\prime\prime}
of edges. It is immediate to verify that
E^{\prime}\subseteq E^{\prime\prime}
(in fact if this does not hold we can simply add the edges of
E^{\prime}
to the set
E^{\prime\prime}
). Moreover, it is easy to see that:

|E^{\prime\prime}|\leq O(\Delta)\cdot\sum_{B\in{\mathcal{B}}}|E^{\prime\prime}(\tilde{B})|\leq O\left((|E^{\prime}|+\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(G))\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n)\right),

as required. In order to complete the proof of Theorem
1.1
, it is now enough to show that there is a drawing
\varphi
of
G
with at most

O\left((|E^{\prime}|+\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(G))\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n)\right)
crossings, such that the edges of
G\setminus E^{\prime\prime}
do not participate in any crossings in
\varphi
. We obtain the drawing
\varphi
by “gluing” together the drawings
\left\{\varphi_{\tilde{B}}\right\}_{\tilde{B}\in{\mathcal{B}}}
(this part is similar to the algorithm of
[
CMS11
]
). Before we prove the existence of the desired drawing
\varphi
of
G
, we set up some notation.

For each pseudo-block
B_{0}\in{\mathcal{B}}
, we denote by
\operatorname{Desc}(B_{0})
the collection of all pseudo-blocks
B_{1}\in{\mathcal{B}}
, such that vertex
v(B_{1})
is the descendant of vertex
v(B_{0})
in the decomposition tree
\tau
. We note that
B_{0}\in\operatorname{Desc}(B_{0})
. Consider now some pseudo-block
B_{0}\in{\mathcal{B}}
and the drawing
\varphi_{\tilde{B}_{0}}
of graph
\tilde{B}_{0}
that we defined above. For simplicity of notation, we denote by
N(\tilde{B}_{0})
the total number of crossings in the drawing
\varphi_{\tilde{B}_{0}}
. We also denote by
N_{0}(\tilde{B}_{0})
the total number of crossings in the drawing
\varphi_{\tilde{B}_{0}}
in which the fake parent-edge
e^{*}_{\tilde{B}_{0}}
participates, and we denote by
N_{1}(\tilde{B}_{0})=N(\tilde{B}_{0})-N_{0}(\tilde{B}_{0})
the total number of all other crossings in
\varphi_{\tilde{B}_{0}}
.
The following lemma allows us to “glue” the drawings
\left\{\varphi_{\tilde{B}}\right\}_{\tilde{B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}}
together.

Lemma 8.5
.

There is an efficient algorithm, that, given a pseudo-block
B_{0}\in{\mathcal{B}}
, and drawings
\left\{\varphi_{\tilde{B}}\right\}_{B\in\operatorname{Desc}(B_{0})}
, computes a drawing
\hat{\varphi}_{B_{0}}
of graph
B_{0}\cup\left\{e^{*}_{\tilde{B}_{0}}\right\}
(if
B_{0}=G
and edge
e^{*}_{\tilde{B}_{0}}
is undefined, then
\hat{\varphi}_{B_{0}}
is a drawing of
B_{0}
), such that the following hold:

•

edges of
E^{\prime\prime}\cap E(B_{0})
do not participate in any crossings in
\hat{\varphi}_{B_{0}}
;

•

the total number of crossings in
\hat{\varphi}_{B_{0}}
in which the fake edge
e^{*}_{\tilde{B}_{0}}
participates is at most
4\Delta N_{0}(\tilde{B}_{0})
; and

•

the total number of all other crossings in
\hat{\varphi}_{B_{0}}
is at most
16\Delta^{2}\left(\sum_{B\in\operatorname{Desc}(B_{0})}N(\tilde{B})-N_{0}(\tilde{B}_{0})\right)
.

We note that Lemma
8.5
provides somewhat stronger guarantees than what is needed for the proof of Theorem
1.1
: it would be sufficient to provide an existential version of the lemma, and the efficient algorithm for constructing the drawing
\hat{\varphi}_{B_{0}}
from the drawings
\left\{\varphi_{\tilde{B}_{1}}\right\}_{B_{1}\in\operatorname{Desc}(B_{0})}
is not needed. But we will also exploit this lemma in the proofs of Theorem
3.5
and Theorem
1.3
, for which the constructive version is necessary.

It is now immediate to obtain the proof of Theorem
1.1
, by applying Lemma
8.5
to the pseudo-block
B_{0}=G
. Let
\hat{\varphi}=\hat{\varphi}_{B_{0}}
denote the resulting drawing of
G
. We are then guaranteed that no edges of
E^{\prime\prime}
participate in crossings in
\hat{\varphi}
, and moreover:

\mathsf{cr}(\hat{\varphi})\leq O\left(\Delta^{2}\cdot\sum_{B_{1}\in\operatorname{Desc}(B_{0})}N(\tilde{B}_{1})\right)\leq O\left(\Delta^{2}\cdot\sum_{B\in{\mathcal{B}}}\mathsf{cr}(\varphi_{\tilde{B}})\right)\leq O\left((|E^{\prime}|+\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(G))\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n)\right).

In order to complete the proof of Theorem
1.1
, it is now enough to prove Lemma
8.5
.

Proof of Lemma
8.5
.

The proof is by induction on the length of the longest path from vertex
v(B_{0})
to a leaf vertex of
\tau
that is a descendant of
v(B_{0})
in
\tau
. The base case is when
v(B_{0})
is a leaf vertex of
\tau
. In this case, graph
\tilde{B}_{0}
is exactly
B_{0}\cup\left\{e^{*}_{\tilde{B}_{0}}\right\}
, and we set
\hat{\varphi}_{B_{0}}=\varphi_{\tilde{B}_{0}}
. From the definition of the set
E^{\prime\prime}
of edges, no edges of
E^{\prime\prime}\cap E(B_{0})
participate in crossings in the resulting drawing; the number of crossings in which edge
e^{*}_{\tilde{B}_{0}}
participates is
N_{0}(\tilde{B}_{0})
, and the total number of all other crossings in
\hat{\varphi}_{B_{0}}
is
N(\tilde{B}_{0})-N_{0}(\tilde{B}_{0})
. Therefore, drawing
\hat{\varphi}_{B_{0}}
has all required properties.

Next, we consider an arbitrary pseudo-block
B_{0}\in{\mathcal{B}}
, where
v(B_{0})
is not a leaf of
\tau
. Let
B_{1},\ldots,B_{r}
be pseudo-blocks whose corresponding vertices
v(B_{i})
are the children of
v(B_{0})
in tree
\tau
.
We denote, for all
1\leq i\leq r
, the endpoints of the block
B_{i}
by
(x_{i},y_{i})
, so that the edge
e^{*}_{\tilde{B}_{i}}
(the fake parent-edge of
\tilde{B}_{i}
) connects
x_{i}
to
y_{i}
. Note that graph
\tilde{B}_{0}
must also contain an edge
e_{i}=(x_{i},y_{i})
. Since parallel edges are not allowed in graph
\tilde{B}_{0}
, edge
e_{i}
may be a real or a fake edge of
\tilde{B}_{0}
, and moreover, it is possible that for
1\leq i<j\leq r
,
e_{i}=e_{j}
.
We use the induction hypothesis in order to compute, for all
1\leq i\leq r
, a drawing
\hat{\varphi}_{B_{i}}
of
B_{i}\cup\left\{e^{*}_{\tilde{B}_{i}}\right\}
with the required properties.

We denote, for all
1\leq i\leq r
, by
G_{i}
the multi-graph
\tilde{B}_{0}\cup\left(\bigcup_{i^{\prime}=1}^{i-1}B_{i^{\prime}}\right)
.
We start with the drawing
\varphi_{0}=\varphi_{\tilde{B}_{0}}
of
\tilde{B}_{0}
, and then perform
r
iterations. The input to the
i
th iteration is a drawing
\varphi_{i-1}
of graph
G_{i-1}
, and the output is a drawing
\varphi_{i}
of
G_{i}
.

We now describe the execution of the
i
th iteration.
Our starting point is the input drawing
\varphi_{i-1}
of graph
G_{i-1}
on the sphere, and the drawing
\hat{\varphi}_{B_{i}}
of
B_{i}\cup e^{*}_{\tilde{B}_{i}}
. Recall that
x_{i},y_{i}
are the endpoints of the block
B_{i}
, and graph
G_{i-1}
contains the edge
e_{i}=(x_{i},y_{i})
. For convenience of notation, we denote the fake parent-edge of
\tilde{B}_{i}
, whose endpoints are also
x_{i},y_{i}
by
e^{\prime}_{i}
. Note that there must be some point
t^{\prime}_{i}
on the image of the edge
e^{\prime}_{i}
in
\hat{\varphi}_{B_{i}}
, such that the segment
\sigma^{\prime}
of the image of
e^{\prime}_{i}
between
x_{i}
and
t^{\prime}_{i}
lies on the boundary of a single face in the drawing
\hat{\varphi}_{B_{i}}
. Let
F^{\prime}
denote this face. We view
\hat{\varphi}_{B_{i}}
as a drawing on the plane, whose outer face is
F^{\prime}
. Similarly, there is a point
t_{i}
on the image of the edge
e_{i}
in the drawing
\varphi_{i-1}
such that the segment
\sigma
of the image of
e_{i}
between
x_{i}
and
t_{i}
lies on the boundary of a single face in
\varphi_{i-1}
; we denote that face by
F
. We view
\varphi_{i-1}
as a drawing in the plane, where face
F
is the outer face. Next, we superimpose the drawings
\varphi_{i-1}
and
\hat{\varphi}_{B_{i}}
in the plane, such that the two resulting drawings are disjoint, except that the image of the vertex
x_{i}
is unified in both drawings, and the faces
F
and
F^{\prime}
of the two drawings correspond to the outer face of this new drawing, that we denote by
F^{*}
. We add a curve
\gamma
to this new drawing, connecting the images of
t_{i}
and
t^{\prime}_{i}
, such that
\gamma
does not intersects any parts of the current drawing, except for its endpoints. The image of the vertex
y_{i}
in the new drawing becomes the image of
y_{i}
in
\varphi_{i-1}
. Let
E_{i}
be the set of all edges of
E(B_{i})
that are incident to
y_{i}
. In order to complete the drawing
\varphi_{i}
of
G_{i}
, we need to define the drawings of the edges in
E_{i}
. Consider any such edge
e=(a,y_{i})
. In order to obtain a new drawing of
e
, we start with the drawing of
e
in
\hat{\varphi}_{B_{i}}
, that connects the image of
a
to the original image of
y_{i}
in
\hat{\varphi}_{B_{i}}
. Next, we follow along the image of the edge
e^{\prime}_{i}
in
\hat{\varphi}_{B_{i}}
, until the point
t^{\prime}_{i}
. Next, we follow the curve
\gamma
, connecting point
t^{\prime}_{i}
to point
t_{i}
, and lastly, we follow the image of the edge
e_{i}
in the drawing
\varphi_{i-1}
, from point
t_{i}
to the image of the vertex
y_{i}
. We can do so in a way that ensures that the images of the edges in
E_{i}
do not cross each other. This defines the final drawing
\varphi_{i}
of the graph
G_{i}
. We now analyze its crossings.

Consider any crossing
(e,e^{\prime})
in
\varphi_{i}
. We say that it is an
old
crossing iff: (i) crossing
(e,e^{\prime})
is present in the drawing
\varphi_{i-1}
; or (ii) crossing
(e,e^{\prime})
is present in the drawing
\hat{\varphi}_{B_{i}}
, and neither of the edges
e,e^{\prime}
is
e^{\prime}_{i}
. All other crossings in
\varphi_{i}
are called
new crossings
. Note that each such new crossing must involve exactly one edge from
E_{i}
. Specifically, for every crossing
(e^{\prime}_{i},\hat{e})
in
\hat{\varphi}_{B_{i}}
, in which the edge
e^{\prime}_{i}
participates, we introduce
|E_{i}|
new crossings of
\hat{e}
with edges of
E_{i}
. Notice that, if
e^{\prime}_{i}
participates in any crossings in
\hat{\varphi}_{B_{i}}
, then
E_{i}\subseteq E^{\prime\prime}
. The number of such new crossings is then bounded, from the induction hypothesis, by
4\Delta N_{0}(\tilde{B}_{i})\cdot|E_{i}|\leq 4\Delta^{2}N_{0}(\tilde{B}_{i})
. Additionally, for every crossing
(e_{i},\hat{e})
in
\varphi_{i-1}
, in which the edge
e_{i}
participates, we introduce
|E_{i}|
new crossings of
\hat{e}
. We say that crossing
(e_{i},\hat{e})
is
responsible
for these new crossings. Our algorithm ensures that crossing
(e_{i},\hat{e})
may only be present in the drawing
\varphi_{i-1}
if edge
e_{i}
participated in crossings in
\varphi_{\tilde{B}_{0}}
. In this case, we are guaranteed that
E_{i}\subseteq E^{\prime\prime}
. Therefore, we ensure that all real edges that participate in the new crossings belong to
E^{\prime\prime}
.
This completes the description of the
i
th iteration.

Let
\hat{\varphi}=\varphi_{r}
be the final drawing of the graph
G_{r}
that we obtain. We now bound the number of crossings in
\hat{\varphi}
. We partition the crossings of
\hat{\varphi}
into three sets: set
\chi_{1}
contains all crossings
(e,e^{\prime})
, where, for some
1\leq i\leq r
,
e,e^{\prime}\in E(B_{i})
. Set
\chi_{2}
contains all crossings
(e,e^{\prime})
, where
e=e^{*}_{\tilde{B}_{0}}
; and set
\chi_{3}
contains all other crossings.

For all
1\leq i\leq r
, let
\chi^{i}_{1}\subseteq\chi_{1}
be the set of all crossings
(e,e^{\prime})
, where
e,e^{\prime}\in E(B_{i})
. From the above discussion, if
(e,e^{\prime})
is a crossing in
\chi^{i}_{1}
, then either it was present in
\hat{\varphi}_{B_{i}}
, or it is one of the new crossings. The number of crossings of the former type is bounded, from the induction hypothesis, by
16\Delta^{2}\left(\sum_{B\in\operatorname{Desc}(B_{i})}N(\tilde{B})-N_{0}(\tilde{B}_{i})\right)
, while the number of crossings of the latter type is bounded by
4\Delta^{2}N_{0}(\tilde{B}_{i})
. Therefore, altogether,
|\chi^{i}_{1}|\leq 16\Delta^{2}\left(\sum_{B\in\operatorname{Desc}(B_{i})}N(\tilde{B})\right)
.

We now proceed to bound the number of crossings in
\chi_{2}
. Consider any crossing
(e,e^{\prime})
in the drawing
\varphi_{\tilde{B}_{0}}
of
\tilde{B}_{0}
, where
e=e^{*}_{\tilde{B}_{0}}
. If
e^{\prime}
is a real edge of
\tilde{B}_{0}
, then this crossing is present in
\chi_{2}
. Otherwise,
e^{\prime}=e_{i}
for some
1\leq i\leq r
. Then crossing
(e,e^{\prime})
is responsible for
|E_{i}|
new crossings in
\chi_{2}
. It is then easy to verify that every crossing
(e,e^{\prime})
of
\varphi_{\tilde{B}_{0}}
with
e=e^{*}_{\tilde{B}_{0}}
may be responsible for at most
\Delta
crossings in
\chi_{2}
, and so
|\chi_{2}|\leq\Delta N_{0}(\tilde{B}_{0})
.

Lastly, we need to bound
|\chi_{3}|
. We partition set
\chi_{3}
of crossings into three subsets: set
\chi_{3}^{\prime}
contains all crossings
(e,e^{\prime})
, where both
e,e^{\prime}
are real edges of
\tilde{B}_{0}
. It is easy to verify that
|\chi_{3}^{\prime}|\leq N_{1}(\tilde{B}_{0})
. Set
\chi_{3}^{\prime\prime}
contains all crossings
(e,e^{\prime})
, where
e
is a real edge of
\tilde{B}_{0}
, and
e^{\prime}\in E(B_{i})
, for some
1\leq i\leq r
. In this case, drawing
\varphi_{i-1}
of
G_{i-1}
contained a crossing
(e,e_{i})
, that was charged for this new crossing, and the total charge to each such crossing
(e,e_{i})
was at most
|E_{i}|
. It is then easy to verify that
|\chi_{3}^{\prime\prime}|\leq\Delta N_{1}(\tilde{B}_{0})
. Lastly, set
\chi_{3}^{\prime\prime\prime}
contains all remaining crossings
(e,e^{\prime})
, where
e\in B_{i},e^{\prime}\in B_{j}
, for some
1\leq i\neq j\leq r
. In this case, it is easy to verify that crossing
(e_{i},e_{j})
must have been present in drawing
\hat{\varphi}_{\tilde{B}_{0}}
of
\tilde{B}_{0}
. Moreover, each such crossing
(e_{i},e_{j})
may be responsible for at most
\Delta^{2}
crossings in
\chi_{3}^{\prime\prime\prime}
, and so, overall
|\chi_{3}^{\prime\prime\prime}|\leq\Delta^{2}N_{1}(\tilde{B}_{0})
. We conclude that
|\chi_{3}|\leq 2\Delta^{2}N_{1}(\tilde{B}_{0})
.

We obtain the final drawing
\hat{\varphi}_{B_{0}}
of
B_{0}\cup\left\{e^{*}_{\tilde{B}_{0}}\right\}
by deleting, from the drawing
\hat{\varphi}
, the images of all fake edges of
\tilde{B}_{0}
, except for the edge
e^{*}_{\tilde{B}_{0}}
. From the above discussion, the total number of crossings in
\hat{\varphi}_{B_{0}}
in which the fake edge
e^{*}_{\tilde{B}_{0}}
participates is at most
4\Delta N_{0}(\tilde{B}_{0})
; and
the total number of all other crossings in
\hat{\varphi}_{B_{0}}
is at most
16\Delta^{2}\left(\sum_{B\in\operatorname{Desc}(B_{0})}N(\tilde{B})-N_{0}(\tilde{B}_{0})\right)
. Moreover, from the discussion above, if edge
e\in E(B_{0})
participates in a crossing in
\hat{\varphi}_{B_{0}}
, then
e\in E^{\prime\prime}
must hold.

8.1
Proof of Theorem
3.5

The proof of Theorem
3.5
follows the algorithm outlined above. We can assume that the input graph
G
is
2
-connected, for the same reason as before, and compute its block decomposition
{\mathcal{B}}={\mathcal{B}}(G)
. Given an input planarizing edge set
E^{\prime}
for
G
, for each pseudo-block
B\in{\mathcal{B}}
, we compute a planarizing edge set
E^{\prime}(\tilde{B})
for
\tilde{B}
exactly as before, so that:

\sum_{B\in{\mathcal{B}}}|E^{\prime}(\tilde{B})|\leq O(|E^{\prime}|+\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(G))
. Since each graph
\tilde{B}
is
3
-connected, we can compute a drawing
\varphi_{\tilde{B}}
of
\tilde{B}
with at most
f(n,\Delta)\cdot(\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(\tilde{B})+|E^{\prime}(\tilde{B})|)
crossings. Applying the algorithm from Lemma
8.5
to the pseudo-block
B=G
, we obtain a drawing of
G
with total number of crossings bounded by:

\begin{split}O(\Delta^{2})\cdot\sum_{B\in{\mathcal{B}}}\mathsf{cr}(\varphi_{\tilde{B}})&\leq O(\Delta^{2})\cdot\sum_{B\in{\mathcal{B}}}f(n,\Delta)\cdot(\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(\tilde{B})+|E^{\prime}(\tilde{B})|)\\
&\leq O\left(\Delta^{2}\cdot f(n,\Delta)(\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(G)+|E^{\prime}|)\right),\end{split}

where we have used Lemma
8.4
in the last inequality.

8.2
Proof of Theorem
1.3

Lastly, we extend our proof of Theorem
1.3
from Section
3.5
to graphs that may not be
3
-connected.

We first compute the block decomposition
{\mathcal{B}}={\mathcal{B}}(G)
of the input graph
G
, and denote
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}=\left\{\tilde{B}\mid B\in{\mathcal{B}}\right\}
. Since each graph
\tilde{B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
is
3
-connected, we can use the algorithm from Section
3.5
to compute, for each graph
\tilde{B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
, an instance
(G_{\tilde{B}},\Sigma_{\tilde{B}})
of the
MCNwRS
problem, such that the number of edges in
G_{\tilde{B}}
is at most
O\left(\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(\tilde{B})\cdot\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n)\right)
, and
\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cnwrs}}(G_{\tilde{B}},\Sigma_{\tilde{B}})\leq O\left(\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(\tilde{B})\cdot\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n)\right)
.
From Lemma
8.4
, the total number of edges in all graphs in
\left\{G_{\tilde{B}}\right\}_{\tilde{B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}}
is at most
O\left(\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(G)\cdot\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n)\right)
, and
\sum_{\tilde{B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}}\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cnwrs}}(G_{\tilde{B}},\Sigma_{\tilde{B}})\leq O\left(\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(G)\cdot\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n)\right)
. We obtain a final instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma)
of the
MCNwRS
problem by letting
G^{\prime}
be the disjoint union of all graphs in

\left\{G_{\tilde{B}}\right\}_{\tilde{B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}}
, and letting
\Sigma=\bigcup_{\tilde{B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}}\Sigma_{\tilde{B}}
.
From the above discussion,
|E(G^{\prime})|\leq O\left(\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(G)\cdot\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n)\right)
, and, since solutions to all instances
(G_{\tilde{B}},\Sigma_{\tilde{B}})
can be combined together to obtain a solution to instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma)
, we get that
\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cnwrs}}(G^{\prime},\Sigma)\leq O\left(\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(G)\cdot\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n)\right)
. Assume now that we are given a solution to instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma)
of
MCNwRS
of value
X
.
This solution immediately provides solutions
\varphi_{\tilde{B}}
to all instances in
\left\{(G_{\tilde{B}},\Sigma_{\tilde{B}})\right\}_{\tilde{B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}}
, such that, if we
denote by
X_{\tilde{B}}
the value of the solution
\varphi_{\tilde{B}}
, then
\sum_{\tilde{B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}}X_{\tilde{B}}\leq X
. Using the algorithm from Section
3.5
, we can compute, for each graph
\tilde{B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
, a drawing
\varphi_{\tilde{B}}
of
\tilde{B}
with at most
O\left(X_{\tilde{B}}+\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(\tilde{B})\right)\cdot\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n)
crossings. By combining these drawings using Lemma
8.5
, we obtain a drawing of
G
, whose number of crossings is bounded by:

O(\Delta^{2})\cdot\sum_{\tilde{B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}}\left(X_{\tilde{B}}+\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(\tilde{B})\right)\cdot\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n)\leq O\left((X+\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(G)\cdot\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n))\right).

9
Crossing Number with Rotation System

In this section we provide the proof of Theorem
1.4
.
Throughout this section, we allow graphs to have parallel edges, but no self-loops. A graph with no parallel edges will be explicitly referred to as a simple graph.
Recall that we are given as input an instance
(G,\Sigma)
of the
MCNwRS
problem, where
G
is a graph, and
\Sigma=\left\{{\mathcal{O}}_{v}\right\}_{v\in V(G)}
is a rotation system for
G
.
Our goal is to compute a drawing of
G
that respects the rotation system
\Sigma
, while minimizing the number of its crossings.
We let
\varphi^{*}
be some fixed optimal solution to input instance
(G,\Sigma)
.
Given a rotation system
\Sigma
for graph
G
, we denote by
\Phi(G,\Sigma)
the set of drawings of
G
that respect the rotation system
\Sigma
.
Throughout this section, we denote
\chi=\mathsf{cr}(\varphi^{*})+|E(G)|
, and fix the value of the constant
\epsilon
from the statement of Theorem
1.4
to be
\epsilon=1/20
.
We will design a randomized algorithm that, with high probability, computes a solution to instance
(G,\Sigma)
of
MCNwRS
, of cost at most
O(\chi^{2-\epsilon}\cdot\operatorname{poly}(\log n))
. Over the course of the algorithm, we consider subinstances
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
of instance
(G,\Sigma)
, but the parameter
\chi
is always defined with respect to the original input instance
(G,\Sigma)
.

9.1
High-Level Overview

We start with a high-level overview of the algorithm.
Our algorithm is recursive. Over the course of the algorithm, we consider subinstances
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
of the input instance
(G,\Sigma)
of
MCNwRS
. Given such a subinstance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
, if maximum vertex degree in graph
G^{\prime}
is bounded by
\chi^{1-\epsilon}
, then we can use known techniques in order to compute a solution for instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
of small enough cost. Otherwise, we compute two subinstances
(G_{1},\Sigma_{1})
and
(G_{2},\Sigma_{2})
of
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
that we solve recursively; graphs
G_{1},G_{2}
are obtained from
G^{\prime}
by deleting some edges (and also possibly contracting some other edges). We then carefully combine the two resulting solutions together, in order to obtain a solution to instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
. The decomposition of graph
G^{\prime}
into graphs
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
is computed as follows. Let
v
be any vertex of
G^{\prime}
whose degree in
G^{\prime}
is at least
\chi^{1-\epsilon}
.
Let
\delta_{G^{\prime}}(v)=\left\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{r}\right\}
be the set of edges incident to
v
in
G^{\prime}
, indexed according to their ordering in
{\mathcal{O}}_{v}\in\Sigma^{\prime}
.
Denote
E_{1}=\left\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{\left\lfloor r/2\right\rfloor}\right\}
and
E_{2}=\left\{e_{\left\lfloor r/2\right\rfloor+1},\ldots,e_{r}\right\}
.
We first split the vertex
v
by replacing it with two vertices,
v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime}
, where
v^{\prime}
is incident to all edges in
E_{1}
, and
v^{\prime\prime}
is incident to all edges in
E_{2}
. Then we compute the minimum-cardinality subset
E^{\prime}
of edges, whose removal separates
v^{\prime}
from
v^{\prime\prime}
in the resulting graph, and consider two cases. The first case is when
|E^{\prime}|
is sufficiently small. In this case, we let
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
be the two subgraphs that are obtained by removing the edges of
E^{\prime}
from
G^{\prime}
. Their corresponding rotation systems
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
are defined in a natural way using the rotation system
\Sigma^{\prime}
. We solve instances
(G_{1},\Sigma_{1})
and
(G_{2},\Sigma_{2})
separately, obtaining solutions
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
, respectively. We “glue” these solution by unifying the images of vertices
v^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime\prime}
, and then carefully insert the images of the edges in
E^{\prime}
, to ensure that we do not create too many crossings.

The second, and the more challenging case, is when
|E^{\prime}|
is large. In this case, from Menger’s Theorem, there is a large set of edge-disjoint paths connecting
v^{\prime}
to
v^{\prime\prime}
.
We show a randomized efficient algorithm to compute a set
{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
containing
3
paths connecting
v^{\prime}
to
v^{\prime\prime}
, that we call a
skeleton
, such that the following hold. First, with high probability, the images of the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
in the optimal solution do not cross each other. Together with the ordering
{\mathcal{O}}_{v}
of the edges of
\delta_{G^{\prime}}(v)
around
v
, this gives us almost complete information about how these paths are drawn with respect to each other in the optimal drawing (the information is “almost” complete since the images of some of these paths may cross themselves in the optimal drawing). Second, we can ensure that with high probability, the edges that belong to each of these paths participate in few crossings in the optimal drawing. Lastly, we can ensure that, for each path
P\in{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
, there are few edges that are incident to the vertices of
P
(this is an over-simplification and is only given here for the sake of intuition).
We then use the skeleton to compute a decomposition of instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
into the subinstances
(G_{1},\Sigma_{1}),(G_{2},\Sigma_{2})
that are solved recursively. We now proceed to describe the algorithm more formally.

9.2
The Algorithm

Recall that we are given as input a graph
G
and a rotation system
\Sigma
for
G
. We fix some optimal drawing
\varphi^{*}\in\Phi(G,\Sigma)
, so
\mathsf{cr}(\varphi^{*})=\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cnwrs}}(G,\Sigma)
. We assume w.l.o.g. that every pair of edges crosses at most once in
\varphi^{*}
. Our goal is to design a randomized algorithm that, with high probability, computes a drawing in
\Phi(G,\Sigma)
with
O(\chi^{2-\epsilon}\cdot\operatorname{poly}(\log n))
crossings, where
\chi=\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cnwrs}}(G,\Sigma)+|E(G)|
.

The algorithm is recursive, and we ensure that the depth of the recursion will be bounded by
O(\chi^{2\epsilon})
.
Over the course of the algorithm, we will consider instances
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
of
MCNwRS
. For each such instance, graph
G^{\prime}
can be obtained from graph
G
by first deleting some of its vertices and edges, and then contracting some edges. Therefore, every edge of
G^{\prime}
corresponds to some edge of
G
, and we treat them as the same edge. We say that every such instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
that our algorithm considers is a
subinstance
of the input instance
(G,\Sigma)
.
Given a subinstance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
, our algorithm will either solve it directly (this is the recursion base) or decompose it into two new subinstances
(G_{1},\Sigma_{1})
,
(G_{2},\Sigma_{2})
, such that
E(G_{1})\cap E(G_{2})=\emptyset
.

Difficult edges and special solutions.

For every subinstance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
, we will define a set
\hat{E}(G^{\prime})\subseteq E(G^{\prime})
of edges of
G
, that we call
difficult edges
. We will eventually ensure that the total number of difficult edges, over all instances that the algorithm considers, is small. Next, we define
special solutions
for the instances
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
that we consider.

Definition.
(Special solutions).

Given a subinstance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
of
(G,\Sigma)
, and a set
\hat{E}(G^{\prime})\subseteq E(G^{\prime})
of difficult edges for
G^{\prime}
, we say that a solution
\varphi_{G^{\prime}}\in\Phi(G^{\prime},\Sigma)
to instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
of
MCNwRS
is a
special solution
(or special drawing), if, for every crossing
(e,e^{\prime})
in
\varphi_{G^{\prime}}
, either the crossing
(e,e^{\prime})
is present in
\varphi^{*}
, or at least one of the edges
e,e^{\prime}
belongs to the set
\hat{E}(G^{\prime})
of difficult edges for
G^{\prime}
.

Throughout the algorithm, we ensure that the following invariants hold for every instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
that we consider, and its corresponding set
\hat{E}(G^{\prime})
of difficult edges:

I1.

|E(G^{\prime})|\geq\chi^{1-2\epsilon}/4

(we will later show that this implies that the number of the recursive levels is bounded by
4\chi^{2\epsilon}
);

I2.

If
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
lies in the
i
th level of the recursion, then
|\hat{E}(G^{\prime})|\leq i\cdot\chi^{1-7\epsilon}
; in particular, since the number of the recursive levels is bounded by
4\chi^{2\epsilon}
,

|\hat{E}(G^{\prime})|\leq 4\chi^{1-5\epsilon}
always holds; and

I3.

There is a special solution to instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
.

We emphasize that the set
\hat{E}(G^{\prime})
of difficult edges is defined based on the optimal solution
\varphi^{*}
to the input instance
(G,\Sigma)
. Therefore, neither the set
\hat{E}(G^{\prime})
nor the special solution
\varphi_{G^{\prime}}
is known to the algorithm; they are only used for the sake of analysis.

The following two lemmas will be used by the algorithm; the proofs are deferred to Section
F.1
and
F.2
of Appendix, respectively.

Lemma 9.1
.

There is an efficient algorithm, that, given an instance
(G,\Sigma)
of
MCNwRS
, such that the maximum vertex degree in
G
is at most
\Delta
, computes a solution to this instance of cost at most
O\left((\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cnwrs}}(G,\Sigma)+\Delta\cdot|E(G)|)\cdot\operatorname{poly}(\log n)\right)
.

Lemma 9.2
.

There is an efficient algorithm, that, given an instance
(H,\Sigma)
of the
MCNwRS
problem, a subset
E^{\prime}\subseteq E(H)
of edges of
H
, and a drawing
\varphi
of graph
H\setminus E^{\prime}
that respects
\Sigma
, computes a drawing
\varphi^{\prime}
of
H
that respects
\Sigma
, such that
\mathsf{cr}(\varphi^{\prime})\leq\mathsf{cr}(\varphi)+|E^{\prime}|\cdot|E(H)|
.

Given a subinstance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
of
(G,\Sigma)
, we denote by
N(G^{\prime})
the total number of crossings
(e,e^{\prime})
in the drawing
\varphi^{*}
of
G
, in which at least one of the edges
e,e^{\prime}
lies in
E(G^{\prime})
.
We obtain the following immediate corollary of Lemma
9.2
, that follows from the definition of special solutions.

Corollary 9.3
.

Let
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
be a subinstance of
(G,\Sigma)
with a set
\hat{E}(G^{\prime})
of difficult edges, for which Invariant
I3
holds. Then there is a solution to instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
of
MCNwRS
of cost at most
N(G^{\prime})+|\hat{E}(G^{\prime})|\cdot|E(G^{\prime})|
.

We now proceed to describe the algorithm. For the initial instance
(G,\Sigma)
of
MCNwRS
, we let the set
\hat{E}(G)
of difficult edges be
\emptyset
. Clearly, Invariants
I1
–
I3
hold for this choice of
\hat{E}(G)
.

We now assume that we are given some subinstance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
of
(G,\Sigma)
, with a set
\hat{E}(G^{\prime})
of difficult edges (we emphasize that the edge set
\hat{E}(G^{\prime})
is not know to the algorithm and is only used in the analysis), such that Invariants
I1
–
I3
hold for this subinstance. If the maximum vertex degree in
G^{\prime}
is at most
\chi^{1-\epsilon}
, then we say that instance

(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
is a
base instance
. Base instances serve as the base of the recursion, that we discuss next.

9.3
Recursion Base

We assume that we are given some subinstance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
of
(G,\Sigma)
, with associated set
\hat{E}(G^{\prime})
of difficult edges (that is not known to the algorithm), such that Invariants
I1
–
I3
hold for this subinstance, and moreover, the maximum vertex degree in
G^{\prime}
is at most
\chi^{1-\epsilon}
.
Recall that, from Corollary
9.3
,
\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cnwrs}}(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})\leq N(G^{\prime})+|\hat{E}(G^{\prime})|\cdot|E(G^{\prime})|
.
We use the algorithm from Lemma
9.1
to compute a solution
\varphi_{G^{\prime}}
to instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
of
MCNwRS
, with the number of crossings bounded by:

\begin{split}&O\left((\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cnwrs}}(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})+\chi^{1-\epsilon}\cdot|E(G^{\prime})|)\cdot\operatorname{poly}(\log n)\right)\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq O\left((N(G^{\prime})+|\hat{E}(G^{\prime})|\cdot|E(G^{\prime})|+\chi^{1-\epsilon}\cdot|E(G^{\prime})|)\cdot\operatorname{poly}(\log n)\right)\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq O\left((N(G^{\prime})+\chi^{1-\epsilon}\cdot|E(G^{\prime})|)\cdot\operatorname{poly}(\log n)\right).\end{split}

Here we have used Invariant
I2
in order to bound
|\hat{E}(G^{\prime})|
.

9.4
Recursion Step

We now assume that we are given a sub-instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
of the input instance
(G,\Sigma)
, together with associated set
\hat{E}(G^{\prime})
of difficult edges in
G^{\prime}
(that is not known to the algorithm), such that Invariants
I1
–
I3
hold for this sub-instance, and, moreover, there is at least one vertex in
G^{\prime}
of degree at least
\chi^{1-\epsilon}
.
Let
v\in V(G^{\prime})
be any vertex of degree at least
\chi^{1-\epsilon}
, and let
\delta_{G^{\prime}}(v)=\left\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{r}\right\}
be the set of all edges that are incident to
v
in
G^{\prime}
, indexed according to the ordering
{\mathcal{O}}_{v}\in\Sigma^{\prime}
.
Denote
E_{1}=\left\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{\left\lfloor r/2\right\rfloor}\right\}
and
E_{2}=\left\{e_{\left\lfloor r/2\right\rfloor+1},\ldots,e_{r}\right\}
.
Let
G^{\prime\prime}
be a graph that is obtained from
G^{\prime}
, by replacing the vertex
v
with two new vertices,
v^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime\prime}
, such that
v^{\prime}
is incident to all edges in
E_{1}
, and
v^{\prime\prime}
is incident to all edges in
E_{2}
. Notice that rotation system
\Sigma^{\prime}
for
G^{\prime}
naturally defines a rotation system
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
for
G^{\prime\prime}
. For convenience, we set
\hat{E}(G^{\prime\prime})=\hat{E}(G^{\prime})
. While instance
(G^{\prime\prime},\Sigma^{\prime\prime})
is not a sub-instance of
G
(that is, we may not be able to obtain graph
G^{\prime\prime}
from graph
G^{\prime}
by only edge-deletion, vertex-deletion and edge-contraction operations), each edge of
G^{\prime\prime}
naturally corresponds to an edge of
G
and Invariants
I1
–
I3
continue to hold for
(G^{\prime\prime},\Sigma^{\prime\prime})
and
\hat{E}(G^{\prime\prime})
.
Consider the special drawing of graph
G^{\prime}
. We can obtain from it a special drawing of graph
G^{\prime\prime}
, by simply replacing the image of vertex
v
with images of
v^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime\prime}
in a natural way. We also define a simple curve
\gamma^{*}
,
that connects the images of
v^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime\prime}
in this new drawing and, other than its endpoints, does not intersect the drawing of
G^{\prime\prime}
. Curve
\gamma^{*}
enters vertex
v^{\prime}
between the edges
e_{1}
and
e_{\left\lfloor r/2\right\rfloor}
, and it enters the vertex
v^{\prime\prime}
between the edges
e_{\left\lfloor r/2\right\rfloor+1}
and
e_{r}
; see Figure
10
for an illustration. We denote the resulting drawing of
G^{\prime\prime}
by
\hat{\varphi}
. For every crossing
(e,e^{\prime})
in this drawing, we say that
(e,e^{\prime})
is a
special crossing
iff at least one of
e,e^{\prime}
lies in the set
\hat{E}(G^{\prime\prime})
of difficult edges; otherwise we say that it is a
regular crossing
.

(a)

Images of edges in
\delta_{G^{\prime}}(v)
in the special solution for instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
.

(b)

Images of edges in
\delta_{G^{\prime\prime}}(v^{\prime})
and
\delta_{G^{\prime\prime}}(v^{\prime\prime})
in drawing
\hat{\varphi}
of
G^{\prime\prime}
, and the curve
\gamma^{*}
.

Figure 10:
Splitting the vertex
v
in
G^{\prime}
into
v^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime\prime}
.

Let
E^{\prime}\subseteq E(G^{\prime\prime})
be a minimum-cardinality set of edges in
G^{\prime\prime}
, such that in
G^{\prime\prime}\setminus E^{\prime}
, no path connects
v^{\prime}
to
v^{\prime\prime}
. We now consider two cases, depending on whether the cardinality of
E^{\prime}
is sufficiently small.

9.4.1
Case 1.
|E^{\prime}|<\chi^{1-2\epsilon}

Consider the graph
G^{\prime\prime}\setminus E^{\prime}
. Let
G_{1}
be the unique connected component of this graph containing the vertex
v^{\prime}
, and let
G_{2}=(G^{\prime\prime}\setminus E^{\prime})\setminus G_{1}
, so
v^{\prime\prime}\in V(G_{2})
. Notice that the rotation system
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
for
G^{\prime\prime}
naturally induces rotation systems
\Sigma_{1}
for
G_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
for
G_{2}
.
We define the sets
\hat{E}(G_{1}),\hat{E}(G_{2})
of difficult edges in the natural way:
\hat{E}(G_{1})=E(G_{1})\cap\hat{E}(G^{\prime})
and
\hat{E}(G_{2})=E(G_{2})\cap\hat{E}(G^{\prime})
. Since the special solution to instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
naturally induces special solutions to instances
(G_{1},\Sigma_{1})
and
(G_{2},\Sigma_{2})
, Invariant
I3
holds for both of these instances. It is immediate to verify that Invariant
I2
also holds for both of these instances. In order to establish Invariant
I1
,
we use the following observation, that provides a slightly stronger bound, that will be useful for us later:

Observation 9.4
.

If Case 1 happens, then
|E(G_{1})|,|E(G_{2})|\geq\chi^{1-\epsilon}/3
.

Proof.

Observe that
|E_{1}|,|E_{2}|\geq\chi^{1-\epsilon}/2-1
, while
|E^{\prime}|\leq\chi^{1-2\epsilon}
. Since
|E(G_{1})|\geq|E_{1}\setminus E^{\prime}|
and
|E(G_{2})|\geq|E_{2}\setminus E^{\prime}|
, the observation follows.

We solve each of the two instances
(G_{1},\Sigma_{1})
and
(G_{2},\Sigma_{2})
recursively, obtaining solutions
\varphi_{G_{1}}
for the first instance and
\varphi_{G_{2}}
for the second instance.
We now show how to combine these solutions together in order to obtain a solution to instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
. Consider the drawing
\varphi_{G_{1}}
of graph
G_{1}
. Since this drawing is a feasible solution to the instance
(G_{1},\Sigma_{1})
, there must be a face
F
in this drawing, whose boundary contains a segment of the image of edge
e_{1}
that includes its endpoint
v^{\prime}
, and a segment of the image of edge
e_{\left\lfloor r/2\right\rfloor}
, that includes its endpoint
v^{\prime}
. We denote this face by
F_{1}
, and we view it as the outer face of drawing
\varphi_{G_{1}}
. Similarly, drawing
\varphi_{G_{2}}
contains a face, that we denote by
F_{2}
, whose boundary contains a segment of the image of edge
e_{\left\lfloor r/2\right\rfloor+1}
that includes its endpoint
v^{\prime\prime}
, and a segment of the image of edge
e_{r}
that includes its endpoint
v^{\prime\prime}
. We view
F_{2}
as the outer face of the drawing
\varphi_{G_{2}}
. We now superimpose the two drawings on the plane, such that (i) the drawing of
G_{2}
is contained in face
F_{1}
of the drawing of
G_{1}
; (ii) the drawing of
G_{1}
is contained in face
F_{2}
of the drawing of
G_{2}
; (iii) the images of vertices
v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime}
coincide, and (iv) the circular order of the edges of
E_{1}\cup E_{2}
entering the image of
v^{\prime}
(and also the image of
v^{\prime\prime}
) is consistent with the ordering
{\mathcal{O}}_{v}\in\Sigma^{\prime}
. Lastly, in order to complete the drawing of the graph
G^{\prime}
, we need to insert the edges of
E^{\prime}
into the current drawing. We do so using the algorithm from Lemma
9.2
. As a result, we obtain a feasible solution
\varphi_{G^{\prime}}
to instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
of
MCNwRS
, such that the number of crossings in
\varphi_{G^{\prime}}
is bounded by:

\mathsf{cr}(\varphi_{G_{1}})+\mathsf{cr}(\varphi_{G_{2}})+|E^{\prime}|\cdot|E(G^{\prime})|\leq\mathsf{cr}(\varphi_{G_{1}})+\mathsf{cr}(\varphi_{G_{2}})+\chi^{1-2\epsilon}\cdot|E(G^{\prime})|.

This finishes the algorithm and the analysis for Case 1.

We now proceed to discuss the second and the more difficult case, where
|E^{\prime}|\geq\chi^{1-2\epsilon}
.

9.4.2
Case 2.
|E^{\prime}|\geq\chi^{1-2\epsilon}

We now focus on the case where
|E^{\prime}|\geq\chi^{1-2\epsilon}
. From Menger’s theorem, graph
G^{\prime\prime}
contains a collection of at least
\chi^{1-2\epsilon}
edge-disjoint paths connecting
v^{\prime}
to
v^{\prime\prime}
. The algorithm for Case 2 consists of three steps. In the first step, we define a set
{\mathcal{P}}
of at least
\chi^{1-2\epsilon}
edge-disjoint paths connecting
v^{\prime}
to
v^{\prime\prime}
in graph
G^{\prime\prime}
, that have some other additional useful properties. In the second step, we construct a skeleton for graph
G^{\prime\prime}
by sub-sampling some of the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}
. In the third step, we define two new sub-instances
(G_{1},\Sigma_{1})
and
(G_{2},\Sigma_{2})
, together with the corresponding sets of difficult edges, that we solve recursively. We then combine the resulting solutions in order to obtain a solution to instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
.

Step 1. Computing the Path Set

We start with some definitions.

Suppose we are given two curves
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}
in the plane, that intersect at a finite number of points, and a point
p
that is an inner point for both
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
. Consider a very small disc
\eta(p)
around
p
, so that the intersection of each of the two curves with
\eta(p)
is a simple contiguous curve. Let
q_{1},q_{1}^{\prime}
be the two points of
\gamma_{1}
that lie on the boundary of
\eta(p)
, and let
q_{2},q_{2}^{\prime}
be defined similarly for
\gamma_{2}
. We say that
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}

cross at point
p
if the circular ordering of the points
q_{1},q_{1}^{\prime},q_{2},q_{2}^{\prime}
along the boundary of
\eta(p)
is either
(q_{1},q_{2},q_{1}^{\prime},q_{2}^{\prime})
or
(q_{1}^{\prime},q_{2},q_{1},q_{2}^{\prime})
(equivalently, if
\sigma
is any curve connecting
q_{1}
to
q_{1}^{\prime}
,
\sigma^{\prime}
is any curve connecting
q_{2}
to
q_{2}^{\prime}
, and both curves are contained in
\eta(p)
, then the two curves must share a point). Otherwise, we say that
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}

touch at point
p
; see Figure
11
for an illustration.
We say that two curves
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}

cross
iff there is at least one point
p
that is an inner point for both curves, such that
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
cross at
p
.

(a)

The curves
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
cross at point
p
.

(b)

The curves
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
touch at point
p
.

Figure 11:
An illustration of a pair of curves that touch or cross.

Assume now that we are given two edge-disjoint paths
P_{1},P_{2}
in graph
G^{\prime\prime}
, and some vertex
u
that is an inner vertex of both
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
. Denote by
e_{1},e_{1}^{\prime}
the two edges of
P_{1}
that are incident to
u
, and similarly denote by
e_{2},e_{2}^{\prime}
the two edges of
P_{2}
that are incident to
u
. We say that the intersection of the paths
P_{1},P_{2}
at vertex
u
is
non-transversal
iff the edges
e_{1},e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2},e_{2}^{\prime}
appear in the ordering
{\mathcal{O}}_{u}\in\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
in one of the following two orders: either (i)
(e_{1},e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2},e_{2}^{\prime})
or (ii)
(e_{1}^{\prime},e_{1},e_{2},e_{2}^{\prime})
(equivalently, in any drawing that respects the rotation system
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
, the images of the two paths touch at
u
). We say that a set
{\mathcal{P}}
of edge-disjoint paths is
non-transversal with respect to
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
,
iff for every pair
P_{1},P_{2}
of distinct paths in
{\mathcal{P}}
, for every vertex
u
that is an inner vertex on both paths, the intersection of
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
at
u
is non-transversal. The first step is summarized in the following lemma, whose proof appears in Appendix
F.3
.

Lemma 9.5
.

There is an efficient algorithm to compute a set
{\mathcal{P}}
of
\left\lceil\chi^{1-2\epsilon}\right\rceil
paths in
G^{\prime\prime}
, such that all paths in
{\mathcal{P}}
are mutually edge-disjoint, each path connects
v^{\prime}
to
v^{\prime\prime}
, and the set
{\mathcal{P}}
of paths is non-transversal with respect to
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
.

We assume without loss of generality that each path in
{\mathcal{P}}
is a simple path. We say that a path
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
is
long
if it contains at least
10\chi^{2\epsilon}
edges.
Since
|E(G^{\prime\prime})|\leq\chi
, the number of long paths in
{\mathcal{P}}
is at most
\chi^{1-2\epsilon}/10
. We let
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}
be the set of all short paths. Additionally, we discard from
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
all paths whose first edge
e_{i}
(that is incident to
v^{\prime}
) has index
1\leq i\leq\chi^{1-2\epsilon}/3
or
\left\lfloor r/2\right\rfloor-\chi^{1-2\epsilon}/3\leq i\leq\left\lfloor r/2\right\rfloor
. Clearly,
|{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}|\geq|{\mathcal{P}}|/4\geq\chi^{1-2\epsilon}/4
(recall that edges
e_{1},\ldots,e_{\left\lfloor r/2\right\rfloor}
are the edges that are incident to vertex
v^{\prime}
, and that
r\geq\chi^{1-2\epsilon}
).

Step 2. Constructing the Skeleton

We use the following two parameters:
h=200\left\lceil\chi^{5\epsilon}\right\rceil

and
q=\left\lfloor\chi^{1-7\epsilon}/1000\right\rfloor
. Note that
|{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}|\geq\chi^{1-2\epsilon}/4\geq hq\geq\chi^{1-2\epsilon}/8
.
We discard arbitrarily all but
hq
paths from
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
, obtaining a subset
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
containing exactly
hq
paths. Notice that the ordering
{\mathcal{O}}_{v^{\prime}}\in\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
of the edges of
\delta_{G^{\prime\prime}}(v^{\prime})
naturally defines an ordering of the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime\prime}
, where the paths are ordered according to the ordering of the first edge on each path in
{\mathcal{O}}_{v^{\prime}}
. We denote this ordering by
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}
.

We choose a path
P\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime\prime}
uniformly at random (this is the only randomized step in the algorithm). We then denote
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime\prime}=\left\{P_{1},P_{2},\ldots,P_{hq}\right\}
, where
P_{1}=P
, and the paths are indexed according to their ordering in
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}
. Next, we partition the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime\prime}
into
q
groups
{\mathcal{U}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{U}}_{q}
, each of which contains exactly
h
consecutive paths of
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime\prime}
, so for
1\leq i\leq q
,
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}=\left\{P_{j}\mid h(i-1)<j\leq hi\right\}
.
We denote
{\mathcal{G}}=\left\{{\mathcal{U}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{U}}_{q}\right\}
the collection of these groups.

For each
1\leq i\leq q
, we define three special paths for group
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
:
we let
Q_{L}^{i}
and
Q^{i}_{R}
be the first and the last paths in group
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
, and we let
Q^{i}
be the “middle” path in that group (that is,
Q^{i}
is the
(h/2)
th path in group
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
). We denote
{\mathcal{Q}}^{i}=\left\{Q^{i}_{L},Q^{i}_{R},Q^{i}\right\}
, and
we let
{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}={\mathcal{Q}}^{1}
. We let the skeleton
K
be the union of the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
.

Next, we define several bad events and show that, with a high enough probability, none of them happens.
Recall that we denoted by
\hat{\varphi}
the special drawing of
G^{\prime\prime}
, and that, for every crossing
(e,e^{\prime})
in this drawing, we say that
(e,e^{\prime})
is a
special crossing
iff at least one of
e,e^{\prime}
lies in the set
\hat{E}(G^{\prime\prime})
of difficult edges, and otherwise we say that it is a
regular crossing
. Clearly, the number of regular drawings in
\hat{\varphi}
is bounded by
\mathsf{cr}(\varphi^{*})\leq\chi
.

Type-1 Bad Groups.

Definition.
.

We say that a group
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
is a
type-
1
bad group
iff there is some path
P\in{\mathcal{Q}}^{i}
, such that:

•

either there are at least
\chi^{5\epsilon}
regular crossings in
\hat{\varphi}
in which the edges of
P
participate; or

•

some edge of
P
belongs to the set
\hat{E}(G^{\prime\prime})
of difficult edges.

Claim 9.6
.

For a fixed index
1\leq i\leq q
, the probability that group
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
is a type-1 bad group is
O(\chi^{-3\epsilon})
.

Proof.

We say that a path
P\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime\prime}
is
bad
if one of the following two happen: either (i) there are at least
\chi^{5\epsilon}
regular crossings in
\hat{\varphi}
in which the edges of
P
participate, or (ii) some edge of
P
belongs to the set
\hat{E}(G^{\prime\prime})
of difficult edges.
Recall that, from Invariant
I2
,
|\hat{E}(G^{\prime\prime})|\leq O(\chi^{1-5\epsilon})
, so at most
O(\chi^{1-5\epsilon})
paths in
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime\prime}
may contain a difficult edge.
Since the total number of regular crossings in
\hat{\varphi}
is at most
\chi
, there are at most
O(\chi^{1-5\epsilon})
paths in
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime\prime}
whose edges participate in at least
\chi^{5\epsilon}
regular crossings in
\hat{\varphi}
. Therefore, the total number of bad paths in
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime\prime}
is bounded by
O(\chi^{1-5\epsilon})
. The probability of any path to be chosen into
{\mathcal{Q}}^{i}
is at most
O(1/(hq))\leq O(1/\chi^{1-2\epsilon})
. Therefore, the probability that a bad path lies in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{i}
is at most
O(\chi^{1-5\epsilon}/\chi^{1-2\epsilon})\leq O(\chi^{-3\epsilon})
.

Type-2 Bad Groups.

Definition.
.

We say that a group
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
is a
type-2 bad group
iff the paths in
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
contain in total at least
50\chi^{5\epsilon}
difficult edges.

Claim 9.7
.

For a fixed index
1\leq i\leq q
, the probability that group
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
is a type-2 bad group is
O(\chi^{-3\epsilon})
.

Proof.

From Invariant
I2
, there are at most
4\chi^{1-5\epsilon}
difficult edges in
\hat{E}(G^{\prime\prime})
. Therefore, the total number of bad groups in
{\mathcal{G}}
is bounded by
\chi^{1-10\epsilon}/10
. Since
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
is essentially a random group from
{\mathcal{G}}
, and since the total number of groups
q=\Omega(\chi^{1-7\epsilon})
, the probability that
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
is a type-2 bad group is at most
O(\chi^{1-10\epsilon}/\chi^{1-7\epsilon})\leq O(\chi^{-3\epsilon})
.

We let
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathcal{G}}
contain all groups
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
that is not a type-1 bad group or a type-2 bad group.
From Claim
9.6
and Claim
9.7
, there is some constant
c
, such that
\text{\bf E}\left[|{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}|\right]\geq(1-\frac{c}{\chi^{3\epsilon}})\cdot q
.

Bad Event
{\cal{E}}_{1}
.

We let
{\cal{E}}_{1}
be the bad event that
|{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}|<\frac{qc}{2\chi^{3\epsilon}}
.

Claim 9.8
.

\text{\bf Pr}\left[{\cal{E}}_{1}\right]\leq\frac{2c}{\chi^{3\epsilon}}
.

Proof.

Assume otherwise. Then:

\text{\bf E}\left[|{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}|\right]\leq\text{\bf Pr}\left[{\cal{E}}_{1}\right]\cdot\frac{cq}{2\chi^{3\epsilon}}+\left(1-\frac{2c}{\chi^{3\epsilon}}\right)\cdot q\leq\frac{cq}{2\chi^{3\epsilon}}+\left(1-\frac{2c}{\chi^{3\epsilon}}\right)\cdot q<\left(1-\frac{c}{\chi^{3\epsilon}}\right)\cdot q,

a contradiction.

We need the following claim.

Claim 9.9
.

Assume that
|{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}|>1
. Then for each group
{\mathcal{U}}^{i}\in{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
and for every pair
P,P^{\prime}
of paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{i}
, the images of the paths
P
and
P^{\prime}
in drawing
\hat{\varphi}
of
G^{\prime\prime}
do not cross (but they may touch at their shared vertices). Moreover, for every pair
{\mathcal{U}}_{i},{\mathcal{U}}_{j}
of distinct and non-consecutive groups in
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
, and for every pair
P\in{\mathcal{Q}}^{i}
,
P^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{Q}}^{j}
of paths, the images of
P
and
P^{\prime}
in drawing
\hat{\varphi}
of
G^{\prime\prime}
do not cross.

Proof.

We start with the first claim. Let
{\mathcal{U}}^{i}\in{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
be a group, and let
P,P^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{U}}^{i}
be a pair of its paths. Assume first that
P=Q_{L}^{i}
and
P^{\prime}=Q^{i}
. Since the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}
are non-transversal with respect to
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
, and since the drawing
\hat{\varphi}
respects the rotation system
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
, if
u
is a vertex that is shared by
P
and
P^{\prime}
, then their images
\hat{\varphi}(P)
and
\hat{\varphi}(P^{\prime})
do not cross at
u
. Therefore, if
\hat{\varphi}(P)
and
\hat{\varphi}(P^{\prime})
cross, there must be a pair of edges
e\in E(P)
,
e^{\prime}\in E(P^{\prime})
, whose images cross in
\hat{\varphi}
. Consider the curve
\hat{\varphi}(P)
, that we view as being directed from
v^{\prime}
to
v^{\prime\prime}
. Let
p
be the first point on this curve that belongs to
\hat{\varphi}(P^{\prime})
and is not the image of a vertex. Let
\gamma
be the segment of
\hat{\varphi}(P)
from
v^{\prime}
to
p
; we delete all loops from
\gamma
so that it becomes a simple curve connecting
v^{\prime}
to
p
. We let
\gamma^{\prime}
be the segment of
\hat{\varphi}(P^{\prime})
from
v^{\prime}
to
p
, and we delete all loops from
\gamma^{\prime}
similarly. Therefore, curves
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
both originate at
\hat{\varphi}(v^{\prime})
and terminate at
p
. They may contain other common points, but they cannot cross at those points. For every vertex
u\neq v^{\prime}
whose image is contained in both
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
, we consider a small disc
\eta(u)
around
u
, and we modify the curves
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
so that they are disjoint from the interior of
\eta(u)
, and instead follow its boundary on either side (see Figure
12
).
Let
\hat{\gamma}
and
\hat{\gamma}^{\prime}
denote the resulting two curves. Then the union of
\hat{\gamma}
and
\hat{\gamma}^{\prime}
defines a closed simple curve that we denote by
\lambda
. Curve
\lambda
partitions the plane into two faces,
F
and
F^{\prime}
. We assume w.l.o.g. that vertex
v^{\prime\prime}
lies on the interior of face
F^{\prime}
. Note that, from our definition of the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{i}
, there are at least
h/2-2\geq 99\chi^{5\epsilon}
paths in
{\mathcal{U}}^{i}
that lie between
P
and
P^{\prime}
in the circular ordering
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}
, and there are at least
99\chi^{5\epsilon}
other paths that lie between
P^{\prime}
and
P
(we think of the orientation of the ordering as being fixed). Therefore, there is a set
\hat{\mathcal{P}}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime\prime}
of at least
99\chi^{5\epsilon}
paths
\hat{P}
, such that
\hat{\varphi}(\hat{P})
intersects the interior of the face
F
. Since vertex
v^{\prime\prime}
lies in the interior of face
F^{\prime}
, the image of every path in
\hat{P}
needs to intersect the curve
\lambda
. Since
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
is not a type-2 bad group, there is a subset
\hat{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}\subseteq\hat{\mathcal{P}}
of at least
49\chi^{5\epsilon}
paths that contain no difficult edges. Note also that, since the edges of
E(P)\cup E(P^{\prime})
participate in at most
2\chi^{5\epsilon}
regular crossings in
\hat{\varphi}
in total (this is since
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
is not a type-1 bad group), at most
2\chi^{5\epsilon}
paths in
\hat{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
may contain edges whose images cross images of edges of
E(P)\cup E(P^{\prime})
. Therefore, there is at least one path
P^{\prime\prime}\in\hat{\mathcal{P}}
, such that
\hat{\varphi}(P^{\prime\prime})
crosses
\lambda
and
P^{\prime\prime}
does not contain an edge whose image crosses the images of edges in
E(P)\cup E(P^{\prime})
. Therefore, the only way for the image of
P^{\prime\prime}
to cross the curve
\lambda
is through some disc
\eta(u)
of some vertex
u\in V(P)\cap V(P^{\prime})
. In particular,
u\in V(P^{\prime\prime})
must hold as well. However, since we have defined the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}
to be non-transversal with respect to the rotation system
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
, and since the drawing
\hat{\varphi}
of
G^{\prime\prime}
must respect the rotation system
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
, this is impossible.

(a)

The curves
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
touch at the image of
u
in
\hat{\varphi}
.

(b)

The curves
\hat{\gamma}
and
\hat{\gamma}^{\prime}
share only endpoints
p
and
\hat{\varphi}(v^{\prime})
.

Figure 12:
Illustration for the proof of Claim
9.9
.

The proof for the case where
\left\{P,P^{\prime}\right\}=\left\{Q^{i},Q^{i}_{R}\right\}
is symmetric, and the proof for the case where
\left\{P,P^{\prime}\right\}=\left\{Q^{i}_{L},Q^{i}_{R}\right\}
follows from these two cases.
The proof for the case where
P\in{\mathcal{Q}}^{i}
,
P^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{Q}}^{j}
for some pair
{\mathcal{U}}_{i},{\mathcal{U}}_{j}\in{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
of distinct and non-consecutive groups of
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
is almost identical (we may need to use a group of
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
that lies between these two groups in order to define the set
\hat{\mathcal{P}}
of paths).

Let
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
be the set of groups that is obtained by discarding every other group from
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
(in other words, no two groups of
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime}
are consecutive in
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
). Consider the drawing
\hat{\varphi}
of
G^{\prime\prime}
and some group
{\mathcal{U}}^{i}\in{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime}
. We define the
region
R^{i}
of the sphere associated with
{\mathcal{U}}^{i}
as follows. We let
\lambda
be the union of two curves: curve
\sigma
whose endpoints are
v^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime\prime}
, that is contained in the image of path
Q^{i}_{L}
in
\hat{\varphi}
, and curve
\sigma^{\prime}
whose endpoints are
v^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime\prime}
