, that is contained in the image of path
Q^{i}_{R}
in
\hat{\varphi}
. We choose the two curves
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
in such a way that a disc whose boundary is
\lambda
contains the drawing of
Q^{i}_{R}\cup Q^{i}_{L}
in
\hat{\varphi}
. Notice that the boundary of this disc is not necessarily a simple curve, since
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
may touch, and curves
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
may be non-simple. We let
R^{i}
denote the resulting discs.

Let
{\mathcal{R}}=\left\{R^{i}\mid{\mathcal{U}}_{i}\in{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime}\right\}
. Note that, from Claim
9.9
, every pair
R^{i},R^{j}
of distinct discs in
{\mathcal{R}}
are disjoint except for possibly sharing points on their boundaries. We let
R^{*}=R^{1}
be the region associated with group
{\mathcal{U}}^{1}
. We now define several additional bad events, and show that each of them only happens with low probability.

Bad Event
{\cal{E}}_{2}
.

We say that bad event
{\cal{E}}_{2}
happens iff
{\mathcal{U}}_{1}
is either a type-1 or a type-2 bad group. From Claims
9.6
and
9.7
, the probability of event
{\cal{E}}_{2}
is bounded by
O(\chi^{-3\epsilon})
. Notice that, if
{\cal{E}}_{2}
does not happen, then
{\mathcal{U}}^{1}\in{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
. We can then also assume that
{\mathcal{U}}^{1}\in{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime}
.

Bad Event
{\cal{E}}_{3}
.

We say that an edge
e\in E(G^{\prime\prime})
is
interfering
if the intersection of the image of
e
in
\hat{\varphi}
with the interior of the region
R^{*}
is non-empty. We denote the set of all interfering edges by
E^{\tiny\mbox{int}}
. We say that bad event
{\cal{E}}_{3}
happens if
|E^{\tiny\mbox{int}}\setminus\hat{E}(G^{\prime\prime})|\geq\chi^{1-7\epsilon}
.

Claim 9.10
.

\text{\bf Pr}\left[{\cal{E}}_{3}\right]\leq O(\chi^{-3\epsilon})
.

Proof.

Note that
\text{\bf Pr}\left[{\cal{E}}_{3}\right]\leq\text{\bf Pr}\left[{\cal{E}}_{3}\mid\neg({\cal{E}}_{1}\vee{\cal{E}}_{2})\right]+\text{\bf Pr}\left[{\cal{E}}_{1}\vee{\cal{E}}_{2}\right]
.
Recall that we have shown that
\text{\bf Pr}\left[{\cal{E}}_{1}\vee{\cal{E}}_{2}\right]\leq O(\chi^{-3\epsilon})
as proved above.
Therefore, it suffices to show that
\text{\bf Pr}\left[{\cal{E}}_{3}\mid\neg({\cal{E}}_{1}\vee{\cal{E}}_{2})\right]\leq O(\chi^{-3\epsilon})
.

Assume now that neither of the events
{\cal{E}}_{1}
,
{\cal{E}}_{2}
happen. Notice that an edge
e\in E(G^{\prime\prime})
may be interfering in one of the two cases: either the image of
e
crosses the image of some edge of
Q^{1}_{L}\cup Q^{1}_{R}
; or the image of
e
is contained in the interior of region
R^{*}
. Since we have assumed that event
{\cal{E}}_{1}
did not happen, group
{\mathcal{U}}_{1}
is not a type-1 bad group. Therefore, the total number of crossings
(e,e^{\prime})
with
e\not\in\hat{E}(G^{\prime\prime})
and
e^{\prime}\in E(Q^{1}_{L}\cup Q^{1}_{R})
is bounded by
O(\chi^{5\epsilon})<\chi^{1-7\epsilon}/2
, as
\epsilon=1/20
. It now remains to show that, if events
{\cal{E}}_{1}
,
{\cal{E}}_{2}
do not happen, then, with probability at least
1-O(\chi^{-3\epsilon})
, the total number of edges
e
whose image in
\hat{\varphi}
is contained in region
R^{*}
is at most
\chi^{1-7\epsilon}/2
. Since we have assumed that the event
{\cal{E}}_{2}
did not happen,
|{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime}|\geq\Omega(q/\chi^{3\epsilon})=\Omega(\chi^{1-10\epsilon})
.
We say that a group
{\mathcal{U}}^{i}\subseteq{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime}
is suspicious if there are at least
\chi^{1-7\epsilon}/2
edges
e\in E(G^{\prime\prime})
, such that the image of
e
in
\hat{\varphi}
is contained in the region
R^{i}
. Since
|E(G^{\prime\prime})|\leq\chi
, the number of suspicious regions in
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime}
is at most
2\chi^{7\epsilon}
. Since
R^{*}
is essentially a random region from
\left\{R^{i}\mid{\mathcal{U}}_{i}\in{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime}\right\}
, the probability that
{\mathcal{U}}^{1}
is suspicious is at most
O(\chi^{7\epsilon}/\chi^{1-10\epsilon})\leq O(\chi^{-3\epsilon})
, as
\epsilon=1/20
.

Bad Event
{\cal{E}}_{4}
.

For all
1\leq i\leq q
, let
Z^{i}=\left(V(Q^{i}_{L})\cup V(Q^{i}_{R})\right)\setminus\left\{v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime}\right\}
. We partition the set
Z^{i}
of vertices into two subsets: set
Z_{1}^{i}
contains all vertices that appear on both
Q^{i}_{L}
and
Q^{i}_{R}
; we refer to vertices of
Z^{i}_{1}
as vertices that are
common
for group
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
; and set
Z^{i}_{2}
that contains all other vertices, that we refer to as vertices that are
uncommon
for group
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
.

Claim 9.11
.

For every vertex
u\in V(G^{\prime\prime})
, there are at most two groups
{\mathcal{U}}_{i},{\mathcal{U}}_{j}\in{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime}
such that
u\in Z_{2}^{i}
and
u\in Z_{2}^{j}
.

Proof.

It is sufficient to show that (i) there is at most one group
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
in
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime}
, such that
u\in V(Q^{i}_{L})
but
u\not\in V(Q^{i}_{R})
; and (ii) there is at most one group
{\mathcal{U}}_{j}
in
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime}
, such that
u\in V(Q^{j}_{R})
but
u\not\in V(Q^{j}_{L})
. We prove the former; the proof of the latter is symmetric.

Assume for contradiction that there are two distinct groups
{\mathcal{U}}_{i},{\mathcal{U}}_{j}\in{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime}
, such that
u\in V(Q^{i}_{L})\setminus V(Q^{i}_{R})
and
u\in V(Q^{j}_{L})\setminus V(Q^{j}_{R})
. Consider the drawing
\hat{\varphi}
of
G^{\prime\prime}
, and delete all edges and vertices from it, except for the edges and vertices of
Q^{i}_{R}\cup Q^{j}_{R}
. Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be the set of faces of the resulting drawing. Then there is some face
F
that contains the image of
u
in its interior. Observe however that the images of every pair of the four paths
Q^{j}_{L},Q^{j}_{R},Q^{i}_{L},Q^{i}_{R}
cannot cross, and can only touch. Moreover, they appear in the ordering
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}
in this order (assuming that
j<i
). Therefore, the removal of the images of
Q^{i}_{R}
and
Q^{j}_{R}
from the drawing
\hat{\varphi}
separates the images of
Q^{i}_{L}
and
Q^{j}_{L}
. It is then impossible that both these paths contain the vertex
u
.

We say that bad event
{\cal{E}}_{4}
happens if the total number of edges of
G^{\prime\prime}
that are incident to vertices of
Z_{2}^{1}
(the uncommon vertices for group
{\mathcal{U}}^{1}
) is at least
\chi^{1-4\epsilon}
.

Claim 9.12
.

\text{\bf Pr}\left[{\cal{E}}_{4}\right]\leq O(\chi^{-3\epsilon})
.

Proof.

Assume that events
{\cal{E}}_{1},{\cal{E}}_{2}
did not happen.
We say that a group
{\mathcal{U}}^{i}\in{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime}
is problematic if the number of edges of
G^{\prime\prime}
that are incident to vertices of
Z^{i}_{2}
is at least
\chi^{1-4\epsilon}
. Notice that, from Claim
9.11
, each edge may be incident to a vertex of
Z^{i}_{2}
for at most four groups
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}\in{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime}
. Since
|E(G^{\prime\prime})|\leq\chi
, at most
O(\chi^{4\epsilon})
groups of
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime}
may be problematic.
Since we assumed that event
{\cal{E}}_{1}
did not happen,
|{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime}|\geq\Omega(q/\chi^{3\epsilon})\geq\Omega(\chi^{1-10\epsilon})
.
Since group
{\mathcal{U}}^{1}
is a random group of
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime\prime}
, the probability that
{\mathcal{U}}^{1}
is problematic is at most
O(\chi^{4\epsilon}/\chi^{1-10\epsilon})\leq O(\chi^{-3\epsilon})
, as
\epsilon=1/20
.
We conclude that
\text{\bf Pr}\left[{\cal{E}}_{4}\mid\neg({\cal{E}}_{1}\vee{\cal{E}}_{2})\right]\leq O(\chi^{-3\epsilon})
. Altogether,

\text{\bf Pr}\left[{\cal{E}}_{4}\right]\leq\text{\bf Pr}\left[{\cal{E}}_{4}\mid\neg({\cal{E}}_{1}\vee{\cal{E}}_{2})\right]+\text{\bf Pr}\left[{\cal{E}}_{1}\vee{\cal{E}}_{2}\right]\leq O(\chi^{-3\epsilon})
.

We let
{\cal{E}}
be the bad event that any of the bad events
{\cal{E}}_{1},{\cal{E}}_{2},{\cal{E}}_{3}
or
{\cal{E}}_{4}
happened. From the above discussion,
\text{\bf Pr}\left[{\cal{E}}\right]\leq O(\chi^{-3\epsilon})
.

From now on we will only focus on group
{\mathcal{U}}_{1}
. We denote
Q^{1}_{L},Q^{1}_{R},Q^{1}
by
Q_{L},Q_{R},Q
, respectively, and we denote
{\mathcal{Q}}^{1}
by
{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
.
We also denote the sets
Z^{1}_{1},Z^{1}_{2}
of common and uncommon vertices for group
{\mathcal{U}}^{1}
by
Z_{1}
and
Z_{2}
, respectively. Recall that the region
R^{1}
associated with
{\mathcal{U}}_{1}
is denoted by
R^{*}
. We now summarize the facts that we need to use in Step 3. Assuming that event
{\cal{E}}
does not happen:

F1.

at most
50\chi^{5\epsilon}
paths in
{\mathcal{U}}_{1}
may contain difficult edges;

F2.

paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
do not contain difficult edges;

F3.

there are at most
3\chi^{5\epsilon}
regular crossings in
\hat{\varphi}
in which the edges of the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
participate;

F4.

the total number of interfering edges (edges whose image intersects the interior of the region
R^{*}
associated with group
{\mathcal{U}}^{1}
) that are non-difficult edges is
|E^{\tiny\mbox{int}}\setminus\hat{E}(G^{\prime\prime})|\leq\chi^{1-7\epsilon}
;

F5.

the total number of edges that are incident to the vertices of
Z_{2}
(the uncommon vertices for
{\mathcal{U}}_{1}
) is bounded by
\chi^{1-4\epsilon}
; and

F6.

each path in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
contains at most
10\chi^{2\epsilon}
edges.

Step 3. Completing the Algorithm

Recall that we have denoted the set of edges that are incident to vertex
v^{\prime}
by
\left\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{\left\lfloor r/2\right\rfloor}\right\}
, where the edges are indexed according to their ordering in
{\mathcal{O}}_{v^{\prime}}\in\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
. Recall that we have ensured that no path in
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
contains any edge
e_{i}
with index
1\leq i\leq\chi^{1-2\epsilon}/3
or
\left\lfloor r/2\right\rfloor-\chi^{1-2\epsilon}/3\leq i\leq\left\lfloor r/2\right\rfloor
. We view the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
as directed from
v^{\prime}
to
v^{\prime\prime}
.
Let
e_{i_{L}}
be the first edge on path
Q_{L}
, and define
e_{i_{R}}
similarly for
Q_{R}
. We denote
E_{L}=\left\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{i_{L}-1}\right\}
and
E_{R}=\left\{e_{i_{R}+1},\ldots,e_{\left\lfloor r/2\right\rfloor}\right\}
. Intuitively, sets
E_{L}
and
E_{R}
contain edges that are incident to
v^{\prime}
, and lie on the left and on the right side of the region
R^{*}
in the drawing
\hat{\varphi}
of
G^{\prime\prime}
, respectively. From the above discussion,
|E_{L}|,|E_{R}|\geq\chi^{1-2\epsilon}/3
.
Let
e_{j_{L}}
be the last edge on path
Q_{L}
, and define
e_{j_{R}}
similarly for
Q_{R}
. We denote
E^{\prime}_{L}=\left\{e_{j_{L}},\ldots,e_{r}\right\}
and
E^{\prime}_{R}=\left\{e_{\left\lfloor r/2\right\rfloor+1},\ldots,e_{j_{R}-1}\right\}
. As before, sets
E^{\prime}_{L}
and
E^{\prime}_{R}
contain edges that are incident to
v^{\prime\prime}
, and lie on the left and the right side of the region
R^{*}
in the drawing
\hat{\varphi}
of
G^{\prime\prime}
, respectively.

It will be convenient for us to slightly modify the graph
G^{\prime\prime}
, by splitting the vertex
v^{\prime}
into three vertices: vertex
v^{\prime}_{L}
, that is incident to the edges in
E_{L}
, vertex
v^{\prime}_{R}
that is incident to all edges in
E_{R}
, and vertex
v^{\prime}_{M}
that is incident to all remaining edges. We split vertex
v^{\prime\prime}
into three vertices
v^{\prime\prime}_{L},v^{\prime\prime}_{R}
and
v^{\prime\prime}_{M}
similarly. We denote the resulting graph by
G^{*}
. We modify the drawing of
\hat{\varphi}
in a natural way to obtain a drawing of
G^{*}
, and we keep the curve
\gamma^{*}
, that now connects vertices
v^{\prime}_{M}
and
v^{\prime\prime}_{M}
, is disjoint from the current drawing except for its endpoints, enters vertex
v^{\prime}_{M}
between
e_{i_{L}}
and
e_{i_{R}}
, and enters vertex
v^{\prime\prime}_{M}
between
e_{j_{L}}
and
e_{j_{R}}
.

In order to provide some intuition, we first consider a simpler special case, where the set
Z_{1}
of common vertices for group
{\mathcal{U}}_{1}
is empty. In other words, the only vertices that paths
Q_{L}
and
Q_{R}
share are
v^{\prime}_{M}
and
v^{\prime\prime}_{M}
.

Special Case:
Z_{1}=\emptyset
.

Consider the drawing
\hat{\varphi}
of
G^{*}
. Delete from this drawing the images of all edges in
E^{\tiny\mbox{int}}
, and the images of the edges of
E(Q_{L})\cup E(Q_{R})
. Since we have assumed that paths
Q_{L},Q_{R}
do not share vertices except for
v^{\prime}_{M}
and
v^{\prime\prime}_{M}
, using the definition of the curve
\gamma^{*}
, it is easy to verify that no path connects a vertex of
\left\{v^{\prime}_{L},v^{\prime\prime}_{L}\right\}
to a vertex of
\left\{v^{\prime}_{R},v^{\prime\prime}_{R}\right\}
in the resulting graph.

Intuitively, we would like to define
G_{1}
to be the union of the connected components of the resulting graph that contain
v^{\prime}_{L}
and
v^{\prime\prime}_{L}
, and
G_{2}
to contain the remainder of the resulting graph. We could then solve the problems on
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
recursively, and combine their solutions as in Case 1. A problem with this approach is that the set
E^{\tiny\mbox{int}}
of interfering edges is unknown to us. However, from the above discussion, we do know that there is a relatively small cut separating vertices of
\left\{v^{\prime}_{L},v^{\prime\prime}_{L}\right\}
from vertices of
\left\{v^{\prime}_{R},v^{\prime\prime}_{R}\right\}
in
G^{*}
if
{\cal{E}}
did not happen. Our algorithm computes such a cut, and then recursively solves the resulting two instances, like in Case 1.

Let
E^{\prime}_{1}
be the set of all edges incident to vertices
v^{\prime}_{M}
and
v^{\prime\prime}_{M}
. Observe that all such edges lie in set
E^{\tiny\mbox{int}}
, and from Fact
F4
and Invariant
I2
,
|E^{\prime}_{1}|\leq|E^{\tiny\mbox{int}}|\leq|E^{\tiny\mbox{int}}\setminus\hat{E}(G^{\prime\prime})|+|\hat{E}(G^{\prime\prime})|\leq 5\chi^{1-5\epsilon}
.
We let
E^{\prime}_{2}
be a minimum-cardinality set of edges, whose removal from
G^{*}\setminus E^{\prime}_{1}
separates vertices of
\left\{v^{\prime}_{L},v^{\prime\prime}_{L}\right\}
from vertices of
\left\{v^{\prime}_{R},v^{\prime\prime}_{R}\right\}
in
G^{*}
. Finally, let
E^{\prime}=E^{\prime}_{1}\cup E^{\prime}_{2}
. The following observation is immediate from the above discussion:

Observation 9.13
.

If bad event
{\cal{E}}
did not happen, then
|E^{\prime}|\leq 15\chi^{1-5\epsilon}
.

Proof.

From the above discussion, the edge set
E^{\tiny\mbox{int}}\cup E(Q_{L})\cup E(Q_{R})
separates
v^{\prime}_{L}
from
v^{\prime}_{R}
in
G^{*}
, and
E^{\prime}_{1}\subseteq E^{\tiny\mbox{int}}
. From Invariant
I2
,
|\hat{E}(G^{\prime\prime})|\leq 4\chi^{1-5\epsilon}
. If
{\cal{E}}
does not happen, then, from Fact
F4
,
|E^{\tiny\mbox{int}}\setminus\hat{E}(G^{\prime\prime})|\leq\chi^{1-7\epsilon}
, and, since paths
Q_{L}
,
Q_{R}
are short,
|E(Q_{L})|,|E(Q_{R})|\leq 10\chi^{2\epsilon}
. Overall, we get that
|E^{\prime}_{2}|\leq 10\chi^{1-5\epsilon}
, so
|E^{\prime}|\leq|E^{\prime}_{1}|+|E^{\prime}_{2}|\leq 15\chi^{1-5\epsilon}
.

Next, we proceed like in Case 1. We define two new instances
(G_{1},\Sigma_{1})
,
(G_{2},\Sigma_{2})
as follows. We let
G_{1}
be a sub-graph of
G^{*}
that contains the connected components of
G^{*}\setminus E^{\prime}
containing
v^{\prime}_{L}
and
v^{\prime\prime}_{L}
, and let
G_{2}
be obtained from
(G^{*}\setminus E^{\prime})\setminus G_{1}
by discarding isolated vertices from it. Notice that
v^{\prime}_{M},v^{\prime\prime}_{M}
are isolated vertices in
G^{*}\setminus E^{\prime}
, since every edge incident to either vertex must lie in the set
E^{\tiny\mbox{int}}
of interfering edges. Therefore, it is easy to verify that
G_{1},G_{2}\subseteq G^{\prime\prime}
, and both graphs are disjoint in their edges. We further modify
G_{1}
by unifying vertices
v^{\prime}_{L}
and
v^{\prime\prime}_{L}
(we refer to the new vertex as
v_{L}
), and we
modify
G_{2}
by unifying vertices
v^{\prime}_{R}
and
v^{\prime\prime}_{R}
, calling the new vertex
v_{R}
. Therefore,
G_{1},G_{2}\subseteq G^{\prime}
now holds. The rotation systems
\Sigma_{1}
,
\Sigma_{2}
of the graphs
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
are defined in a natural way using the rotation system
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
. We also define the sets of difficult edges in
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
as
\hat{E}(G_{1})=\hat{E}(G^{\prime\prime})\cap E(G_{1})
and
\hat{E}(G_{2})=\hat{E}(G^{\prime\prime})\cap E(G_{2})
.

We now verify that all invariants hold for the resulting two instances.
First, recall that
|E_{L}|,|E_{R}|\geq\chi^{1-2\epsilon}/3
, while, from Observation
9.13
, if bad event
{\cal{E}}
did not happen, then
|E^{\prime}|\leq 15\chi^{1-5\epsilon}
. Therefore, if bad event
{\cal{E}}
did not happen, then
|E(G_{1})|\geq|E_{L}\setminus E^{\prime}|\geq\chi^{1-2\epsilon}/4
, and similarly
|E(G_{2})|\geq\chi^{1-2\epsilon}/4
. Therefore, Invariant
I1
holds for both instances.
It is also immediate to verify that Invariant
I2
holds for both instances. Lastly, it remains to show that both resulting instances have special solutions. We show this for instance
(G_{1},\Sigma_{1})
, the proof for the other instance is symmetric.
Since
G_{1}\subseteq G^{\prime}
, and
\hat{E}(G_{1})=\hat{E}(G^{\prime\prime})\cap E(G_{1})
, we can obtain a special solution for instance
(G_{1},\Sigma_{1})
, by starting from a special solution for instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
, and erasing from it all vertices and edges of
G^{\prime}
that do not lie in
G_{1}
. We conclude that Invariant
I3
holds for both instances.

We solve each of the two instances
(G_{1},\Sigma_{1})
and
(G_{2},\Sigma_{2})
recursively, obtaining solutions
\varphi_{G_{1}}
for the first instance and
\varphi_{G_{2}}
for the second instance.
We now show how to combine these solutions together in order to obtain a solution to instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
.
The algorithm for combining the two instances is almost identical to the one used in Case 1.
Consider the drawing
\varphi_{G_{1}}
of graph
G_{1}
. Since this drawing is a feasible solution to the instance
(G_{1},\Sigma_{1})
, there must be a face
F
in this drawing, whose boundary contains a segment of the image of edge
e_{i_{L}-1}
that includes its endpoint
v_{L}
, and a segment of the image of edge
e_{j_{L}}
, that includes its endpoint
v_{L}
. We denote this face by
F_{1}
, and we view it as the outer face of drawing
\varphi_{G_{1}}
. Similarly, drawing
\varphi_{G_{2}}
contains a face, that we denote by
F_{2}
, whose boundary contains a segment of the image of edge
e_{i_{R}+1}
that includes its endpoint
v_{R}
, and a segment of the image of edge
e_{j_{R}-1}
that includes its endpoint
v_{R}
. We view
F_{2}
as the outer face of the drawing
\varphi_{G_{2}}
. We now superimpose the two drawings on the plane, such that (i) the drawing of
G_{2}
is contained in face
F_{1}
of the drawing of
G_{1}
; (ii) the drawing of
G_{1}
is contained in face
F_{2}
of the drawing of
G_{2}
; (iii) the images of vertices
v_{L},v_{R}
coincide, and (iv) the circular order of the edges of
E_{L}\cup E^{\prime}_{L}\cup E_{R}\cup E^{\prime}_{R}
entering the image of
v_{L}
(and also the image of
v_{R}
) is consistent with the ordering
{\mathcal{O}}_{v}\in\Sigma^{\prime}
. Lastly, in order to complete the drawing of the graph
G^{\prime}
, we need to insert the edges of
E^{\prime}
into the current drawing. We do so using the algorithm from Lemma
9.2
. As a result, we obtain a feasible solution
\varphi_{G^{\prime}}
to instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
of
MCNwRS
, such that the number of crossings in
\varphi_{G^{\prime}}
is bounded by:

\mathsf{cr}(\varphi_{G_{1}})+\mathsf{cr}(\varphi_{G_{2}})+|E^{\prime}|\cdot|E(G^{\prime})|.

If event
{\cal{E}}
did not happen, then
|E^{\prime}|\leq 15\chi^{1-5\epsilon}
, and the number of crossings is bounded by:

\mathsf{cr}(\varphi_{G_{1}})+\mathsf{cr}(\varphi_{G_{2}})+15\chi^{1-5\epsilon}\cdot|E(G^{\prime})|.

This completes the algorithm for the special case where
Z_{1}=\emptyset
. We now proceed to discuss the general case.

The General Case.

We start with some intuition for the general case. Suppose that the set
Z_{1}
of common vertices is non-empty. Notice that now deleting the set
E^{\tiny\mbox{int}}\cup\hat{E}(G^{\prime\prime})\cup E(Q_{L})\cup E(Q_{R})
of edges from graph
G^{*}
may no longer separate vertices in set
\left\{v^{\prime}_{L},v^{\prime\prime}_{L}\right\}
from vertices in set
\left\{v^{\prime}_{R},v^{\prime\prime}_{R}\right\}
and so it is not immediately clear how to define the two new sub-instances
(G_{1},\Sigma_{1}),(G_{2},\Sigma_{2})
. In order to overcome this difficulty, we carefully
split
every vertex
u\in Z_{1}
, by creating two copies of this vertex. The set of edges incident to
u
is split between the two copies, so each edge is incident to at most one copy. Intuitively, we would like one copy of
u
to be incident to all edges of
\delta_{G^{\prime\prime}}(u)
, whose images lie “to the left” of the region
R^{*}
, and the other copy to be incident to edges whose images lie “to the right” of the region
R^{*}
in
\hat{\varphi}
. We define these notions formally below. Once we split the vertices of
Z_{1}
, we set up a minimum-cut problem in the resulting graph (somewhat similar to the special case we discussed above), and use this cut to define the two initial graphs
G_{1}^{\prime},G_{2}^{\prime}
. Notice, however, that, if we obtain solutions
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}
to instances associated with graphs
G_{1}^{\prime},G_{2}^{\prime}
, then we need to be able to “glue” the copies of all vertices in
Z_{1}
to each other in order to obtain a drawing of
G^{\prime\prime}
. In order to do this, we need to ensure that the copies of the vertices in
Z_{1}
appear on the boundary of a single face in both drawings, and moreover, the order in which they appear on the boundaries of these faces is identical in both drawings. In order to ensure this, we modify the graphs
G_{1}^{\prime},G_{2}^{\prime}
, by unifying the copies of all vertices in
Z_{1}\cup\left\{v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime}\right\}
in both graphs, and carefully define orderings of edges incident to this new vertex in each of the two instances. This is done in a way that ensures that any feasible solutions to the resulting two instances can be composed together in order to obtain a feasible solution to instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
. We now provide a formal description of the algorithm. The main hurdle in the algorithm for the general case is handling the common vertices in
Z_{1}
. We start with some definitions and observations that allow us to do so.

Dealing with Common Vertices.

Consider the drawing
\hat{\varphi}
of the graph
G^{\prime\prime}
. We denote by
\zeta
the image of the path
Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
in
\hat{\varphi}
. Notice that curve
\zeta
may cross itself numerous times. Let
\zeta^{\prime}
be a simple curve, that is obtained from
\zeta
by deleting all loops. The following claim is crucial for dealing with common vertices.

Claim 9.14
.

If event
{\cal{E}}
does not happen, then the image of every common vertex of
Z_{1}
appears on the curve
\zeta^{\prime}
in the drawing
\hat{\varphi}
.

Proof.

Assume that this is not the case. Then there is some loop
\sigma
on the curve
\zeta
that contains the image of some vertex
u\in Z_{1}
. If we denote by
p
the unique point of
\sigma
that lies on
\zeta^{\prime}
, then
u
is not mapped to
p
in
\hat{\varphi}
. By further decomposing
\sigma
into smaller loops, we can find a simple closed curve
\sigma^{\prime}\subseteq\sigma
, such that
u
lies on the boundary of
\sigma^{\prime}
. Let
D
be a disc, whose boundary is
\sigma^{\prime}
, such that the image of
v^{\prime}
does not lie in
D
in
\hat{\varphi}
.

Recall that vertex
u
lies on both paths
Q_{L}
and
Q_{R}
. Recall also that in
{\mathcal{U}}_{1}
, there is a set
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}_{1}
of
99\chi^{5\epsilon}
paths that lie between
Q_{L}
and
Q
(excluding
Q
and including
Q_{L}
), and similarly, there is a set
{\mathcal{S}}_{2}\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}_{2}
of
99\chi^{5\epsilon}
paths that lie between
Q
and
Q_{R}
(excluding
Q
and including
Q_{R}
). If
{\cal{E}}
does not happen, then from Properties
F1
,
F2
and
F3
, at most
50\chi^{5\epsilon}
paths in
{\mathcal{U}}_{1}
may contain difficult edges, paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
do not contain difficult edges, and there are at most
3\chi^{5\epsilon}
regular crossings in
\hat{\varphi}
in which the edges of the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
participate. Therefore, there are at most
53\chi^{5\epsilon}
paths
P\in{\mathcal{U}}_{1}
, such that the image of
P
may cross the images of any of the paths
Q,Q_{L},Q_{R}
in
\hat{\varphi}
(note that, since the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
are non-transversal with respect to
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
, and drawing
\hat{\varphi}
respects
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
, each such crossing of images of two paths must involve a crossing of images of two edges). Therefore, there is a subset
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}_{1}\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
of at least
25\chi^{5\epsilon}
paths, and a subset
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}_{2}\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}_{2}
of at least
25\chi^{5\epsilon}
paths, such that for every path
P\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}_{2}
, the image of
P
does not cross the images of any of the paths
Q,Q_{L},Q_{R}
in
\hat{\varphi}
. It is then easy to see that the image of
P
must be contained in the region
R^{*}
, and moreover, since
u\in Q_{L}\cap Q_{R}
, vertex
u
must lie on
P
.

Let
e,e^{\prime}
be the two edges on path
Q
that are incident to
u
. Consider the ordering
{\mathcal{O}}_{u}\in\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
of the edges that are incident to
u
. Removing the edges
e,e^{\prime}
from this ordering splits the set
\delta_{G^{\prime\prime}}(u)\setminus\left\{e,e^{\prime}\right\}
of edges into two subsets: set
\delta^{1}
of edges lying between
e
and
e^{\prime}
, and set
\delta^{2}
of edges lying between
e^{\prime}
and
e
in
{\mathcal{O}}_{u}
.

Let
\delta_{L}\subseteq\delta(u)
be the set of all edges that are incident to
u
and lie on the paths in
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}_{1}
. Define
\delta_{R}
similarly for path set
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}_{2}
. Since the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
are non-transversal with respect to
{\mathcal{O}}_{u}
, and since the images of the paths in
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{\prime}\cup{\mathcal{S}}_{2}^{\prime}
may not cross the image of
Q
, we are guaranteed that either
\delta_{L}\subseteq\delta^{1}
and
\delta_{R}\subseteq\delta^{2}
, or the other way around. We assume without loss of generality that it is the former.

Depending on the orientation of the vertex
u
in
\hat{\varphi}
, it then must be the case that for at least one of the sets
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{\prime},{\mathcal{S}}_{2}^{\prime}
of paths, the image of every path in the set must have a non-empty intersection with the interior of the disc
D
. We assume without loss of generality that it is
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}_{1}
. Therefore, for each path
P\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}_{1}
, its image contains the image of the vertex
v^{\prime}
, that lies outside disc
D
, and it contains a point that lies strictly inside disc
D
. However, the image of path
P
may not cross the image of path
Q
, a contradiction.

Denote the vertices in
Z_{1}
by
u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{|Z_{1}|}
, where the vertices are indexed in the order of their appearance on path
Q
. It is then immediate to see that the images of the vertices
u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{|Z_{1}|}
appear in this order on the curve
\zeta^{\prime}
.

Consider now some common vertex
u\in Z_{1}
. We view the paths
Q_{L}
,
Q
, and
Q_{R}
as directed from
v^{\prime}
towards
v^{\prime\prime}
. We denote by
e_{L}(u)
the first edge of
Q_{L}
that is incident to
u
, and by
e_{L}^{\prime}(u)
the second such edge. We define the edges
e_{R}(u),e^{\prime}_{R}(u)
for path
Q_{R}
, and
e_{M}(u),e^{\prime}_{M}(u)
for path
Q
similarly. Since all paths in
{\mathcal{P}}
are non-transversal with respect to the ordering
{\mathcal{O}}_{u}\in\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
, the circular ordering of these edges in
{\mathcal{O}}_{u}
is:
(e_{M}(u),e_{L}(u),e^{\prime}_{L}(u),e^{\prime}_{M}(u),e^{\prime}_{R}(u),e_{R}(u))
. We denote by
E_{L}(u)
the set of all edges that lie between
e_{L}(u)
and
e^{\prime}_{L}(u)
(including these two edges). We define an ordering
{\mathcal{J}}^{L}_{u}
of the edges in
E_{L}(u)
to be consistent with
{\mathcal{O}}_{u}
, with edge
e_{L}(u)
appearing first, and edge
e^{\prime}_{L}(u)
appearing last in this ordering (we note that
{\mathcal{J}}^{L}_{u}
is no longer a circular ordering). We denote by
E_{R}(u)
the set of all edges in
\delta_{G^{\prime\prime}}(u)
that lie between
e^{\prime}_{R}(u)
and
e_{R}(u)
in
{\mathcal{O}}_{u}
(including these two edges). We define an ordering
{\mathcal{J}}^{R}_{u}
of the edges in
E_{R}(u)
to be consistent with
{\mathcal{O}}_{u}
, with edge
e_{R}(u)
appearing first, and edge
e^{\prime}_{R}(u)
appearing last in this ordering;
see Figure
13
for an illustration.

(a)

Paths
Q_{L}
,
Q_{R}
and edge sets at vertices
u,u^{\prime}\in Z_{1}
.

(b)

Zoom in at vertex
u
in
(a)
.

Figure 13:
Edge subsets for vertices in
Z_{1}
and their orderings.

Recall that we have defined sets
E_{L},E_{R},E^{\prime}_{L},E^{\prime}_{R}
of edges that are incident to vertices
v^{\prime}_{L},v^{\prime}_{R},v^{\prime\prime}_{L}
and
v^{\prime\prime}_{R}
, respectively. We let
{\mathcal{J}}_{L}
be the ordering of the edges of
E_{L}
, that is consistent with the ordering
{\mathcal{O}}_{v^{\prime}}\in\Sigma^{\prime}
, such that edge
e_{1}
appears first in this ordering. We define ordering
{\mathcal{J}}^{\prime}_{L}
for edges in
E^{\prime}_{L}
similarly, where edge
e_{r}
appears last in the ordering. We similarly define orderings
{\mathcal{J}}_{R}
of edges in
E_{R}
, where edge
e_{\left\lfloor r/2\right\rfloor}
appears last in the ordering, and
{\mathcal{J}}^{\prime}_{R}
of edges in
E^{\prime}_{R}
, where edge
e_{\left\lfloor r/2\right\rfloor+1}
appears first in the ordering.

Lastly, we set
E^{*}_{L}=E_{L}\cup E^{\prime}_{L}\cup\left(\bigcup_{u\in Z_{1}}E_{L}(u)\right)
, and similarly we set
E^{*}_{R}=E_{R}\cup E^{\prime}_{R}\cup\left(\bigcup_{u\in Z_{1}}E_{R}(u)\right)
. We define an ordering
{\mathcal{J}}^{*}_{L}
of the edges in
E^{*}_{L}
as follows. We place the edges in the sets
E_{L},E_{L}(u_{1})
,
E_{L}(u_{2}),\ldots
,
E_{L}(u_{|Z_{1}|})
,
E^{\prime}_{L}
in this order, where the ordering of the edges within each set is identical to the ones we just defined: edges of
E_{L}
and
E^{\prime}_{L}
are ordered according to the orderings
{\mathcal{J}}_{L}
and
{\mathcal{J}}^{\prime}_{L}
, respectively, and for each
1\leq i\leq|Z_{1}|
, edges of
E_{L}(u_{i})
are ordered according to
{\mathcal{J}}^{L}(u_{i})
.
We define an ordering
{\mathcal{J}}_{R}
of the edges in
E^{*}_{R}
similarly. The following observation follows from our discussion so far:

Observation 9.15
.

Suppose we follow the curve
\zeta^{\prime}
from
v^{\prime}
to
v^{\prime\prime}
, immediately to the right of the curve, in drawing
\hat{\varphi}
of
G^{\prime\prime}
. Then we encounter the edges of
E^{*}_{L}
in the order
{\mathcal{J}}^{*}_{L}
. Similarly, if we follow the curve
\zeta^{\prime}
from
v^{\prime}
to
v^{\prime\prime}
immediately to the left of the curve, then we encounter the edges of
E^{*}_{R}
in the order
{\mathcal{J}}^{*}_{R}
.

Constructing the Two Subinstances.

We define the set
E^{\prime}_{1}
of edges that we delete from
G^{*}
, as follows. Set
E^{\prime}_{1}
contains, for every vertex
u\in Z_{2}
, all edges incident to
u
.
Recall that, if
{\cal{E}}
does not happen, then
|E_{1}^{\prime}|\leq\chi^{1-4\epsilon}
, from Fact
F5
.
Let
E^{\prime}_{2}
be a set of edges from
G^{*}
that contains, for every vertex
u_{i}\in Z_{1}
, all edges that are incident to
u_{i}
, except for the edges of
E_{L}(u_{i})\cup E_{R}(u_{i})
. Notice that every edge in
E^{\prime}_{2}
is an interfering edge, that lies in
E^{\tiny\mbox{int}}
. From Fact
F4
, if
{\cal{E}}
did not happen, then
|E^{\tiny\mbox{int}}|\leq\chi^{1-7\epsilon}+|\hat{E}(G^{\prime\prime})|\leq 5\chi^{1-5\epsilon}
(from Invariant
I2
).

Next, we define a graph
\hat{G}^{*}
, as follows. Initially, we let
\hat{G}^{*}=G^{*}\setminus(E^{\prime}_{1}\cup E^{\prime}_{2})
. Next, we consider every vertex
u_{i}\in Z_{1}
one-by-one. When vertex
u_{i}
is considered, we split it into two vertices: vertex
u_{i}^{\prime}
that is incident to all edges in
E_{L}(u_{i})
, and vertex
u_{i}^{\prime\prime}
, that is incident to all edges in
E_{R}(u_{i})
. Once all vertices in
Z_{1}
are processed, we obtain the final graph
\hat{G}^{*}
. We denote
S_{L}=\left\{u^{\prime}_{i}\mid u_{i}\in Z_{1}\right\}\cup\left\{v^{\prime}_{L},v^{\prime\prime}_{L}\right\}
, and we denote
S_{R}=\left\{u^{\prime\prime}_{i}\mid u_{i}\in Z_{1}\right\}\cup\left\{v^{\prime}_{R},v^{\prime\prime}_{R}\right\}
.

Drawing
\hat{\varphi}
of
G^{\prime\prime}
naturally defines a drawing of the resulting graph
\hat{G}^{*}
, that we denote by
\hat{\varphi}^{*}
. It is easy to verify, using this drawing, that the removal of all interfering edges from
\hat{G}^{*}
, together with the edges of
E(Q_{L})\cup E(Q_{R})
, disconnects vertices of
S_{L}
from vertices of
S_{R}
in
\hat{G}^{*}
.

Next, we let
E^{\prime}_{3}
be a minimum-cardinality set of edges, whose removal from
\hat{G}^{*}
disconnects vertices of
S_{L}
from vertices of
S_{R}
. We need the following observation:

Observation 9.16
.

If bad event
{\cal{E}}
did not happen, then
|E^{\prime}_{3}|\leq 5\chi^{1-5\epsilon}
.

Proof.

From the above discussion, the edge set
E^{\tiny\mbox{int}}\cup E(Q_{L})\cup E(Q_{R})
separates
v^{\prime}_{L}
from
v^{\prime}_{R}
in
G^{*}
. From Invariant
I2
,
|\hat{E}(G^{\prime\prime})|\leq 4\chi^{1-5\epsilon}
. If
{\cal{E}}
does not happen, then
|E^{\tiny\mbox{int}}\setminus\hat{E}(G^{\prime\prime})|\leq\chi^{1-7\epsilon}
, and, since paths
Q_{L}
,
Q_{R}
are short,
|E(Q_{L})|,|E(Q_{R})|\leq 10\chi^{2\epsilon}
. Overall, we get that
|E^{\prime}_{3}|\leq 5\chi^{1-5\epsilon}
.

Let
E^{\prime}=E_{1}^{\prime}\cup E_{2}^{\prime}\cup E_{3}^{\prime}
; we refer to the edges of
E^{\prime}
as
deleted edges
. From the above discussion, if
{\cal{E}}
did not happen, then
|E^{\prime}|\leq 2\chi^{1-4\epsilon}
.
We let
G^{\prime}_{1}\subseteq\hat{G}^{*}
be the union of all connected components that contain vertices of
S^{L}
, and we let
G^{\prime}_{2}=(\hat{G}^{*}\setminus E^{\prime})\setminus G^{\prime}_{1}
. We obtain instance
(G_{1},\Sigma_{1})
as follows. Graph
G_{1}
is obtained from graph
G_{1}^{\prime}
, by contracting all vertices in
S_{L}
into a single vertex that we denote it by
v^{*}_{L}
. Notice that the set of edges incident to
v^{*}_{L}
in
G_{1}
is
E^{*}_{L}\setminus E^{\prime}
. The ordering
{\mathcal{O}}_{v^{*}_{L}}
of edges incident to
v^{*}_{L}
is a circular ordering obtained from
{\mathcal{J}}^{*}_{L}
. For every other vertex
u\in V(G_{1})\setminus\left\{v^{*}_{L}\right\}
, vertex
u
lies in
G^{\prime\prime}
, and the set of edges incident to
u
in
G_{1}
is a subset of
\delta_{G^{\prime\prime}}(u)
. We define the ordering
{\mathcal{O}}_{u}
of the edges in
\delta_{G_{1}}(u)
to be consistent with the same ordering in
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
. This defines the rotation system
\Sigma_{1}
for graph
G_{1}
. We define instance
(G_{2},\Sigma_{2})
similarly. We also define the sets of difficult edges:
\hat{E}(G_{1})=(\hat{E}(G^{\prime\prime})\cup E^{\tiny\mbox{int}})\cap E(G_{1})
, and
\hat{E}(G_{2})=(\hat{E}(G^{\prime\prime})\cup E^{\tiny\mbox{int}})\cap E(G_{2})
.

Next, we verify that all invariants hold for the resulting two instances.
First, recall that
|E_{L}|,|E_{R}|\geq\chi^{1-2\epsilon}/3
, while, if bad event
{\cal{E}}
did not happen, then
|E^{\prime}|\leq 2\chi^{1-4\epsilon}
. Therefore, if bad event
{\cal{E}}
did not happen, then
|E(G_{1})|\geq|E_{L}\setminus E^{\prime}|\geq\chi^{1-2\epsilon}/4
, and similarly
|E(G_{2})|\geq\chi^{1-2\epsilon}/4
. Therefore, Invariant
I1
holds for both instances.

If event
{\cal{E}}
did not happen, then, from Fact
F4
,
|E^{\tiny\mbox{int}}\setminus\hat{E}(G^{\prime\prime})|\leq\chi^{1-7\epsilon}
. Therefore,
|\hat{E}(G_{1})|,|\hat{E}(G_{2})|\leq\chi^{1-7\epsilon}+|\hat{E}(G^{\prime})|
, and Invariant
I2
holds for both instances. Lastly, it remains to show that both resulting instances have special solutions. We show this for instance
(G_{1},\Sigma_{1})
, and the proof for the other instance is symmetric.

We start from the drawing
\hat{\varphi}^{*}
of graph
\hat{G}^{*}
, that was obtained from the special solution
\hat{\varphi}
for instance
(G^{\prime\prime},\Sigma^{\prime\prime})
by deleting the edges in
E^{\prime}
from it, and splitting the vertices of
Z_{1}\cup\left\{v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime}\right\}
. We delete from this drawing all edges except those in the set
E(G_{1})\setminus\hat{E}(G_{1})
. Notice that the only crossings in the current drawing are regular crossings of
\hat{\varphi}
– that is, crossings that are present in the optimal drawing
\varphi^{*}
of
G
. In order to obtain a drawing of graph
G_{1}
, we need to contract all vertices in
S_{L}
into a single vertex
v^{*}_{L}
. We show that we can do so without introducing any new crossings. Recall that we have defined a simple curve
\zeta^{\prime}
that is contained in the image of the path
Q
in
\hat{\varphi}
, and the images of the vertices
v^{\prime},u_{1},\ldots,u_{Z_{1}},v^{\prime\prime}
appear on this curve in
\hat{\varphi}
in this order. Recall that the only edges whose images cross curve
\zeta^{\prime}
are edges of
E^{\tiny\mbox{int}}
. We slightly shift the curve
\zeta^{\prime}
to the left, so it contains the images of vertices
v^{\prime}_{L},u^{\prime}_{1},u^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,u^{\prime}_{Z_{1}},v^{\prime\prime}_{L}
in this order. Notice that no edge crosses this new curve
\zeta^{\prime}
in the current drawing, as we have deleted all edges of
E^{\tiny\mbox{int}}
from it. Therefore, we can contract the vertices of
S_{L}
into a single vertex without introducing any new crossings.

Combining the Solutions.

We solve instances
(G_{1},\Sigma_{1}),(G_{2},\Sigma_{2})
recursively, obtaining solutions
\varphi_{G_{1}}
to the first instance and
\varphi_{G_{2}}
to the second instance. We now show how to combine these solutions in order to obtain a solution
\varphi_{G^{\prime}}
to instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
.

Consider the drawing
\varphi_{G_{1}}
of graph
G_{1}
. Let
\eta(v^{*}_{L})
be a small disc around vertex
v^{*}_{L}
in this drawing. For every edge
e\in E^{*}_{L}
, we let
p_{e}
be the unique point lying in the intersection of the image of
e
and the boundary of
\eta(v^{*}_{L})
. Notice that we are guaranteed that the circular ordering of the points in
\left\{p_{e}\mid e\in E^{*}_{L}\right\}
on the boundary of
\eta(v^{*}_{L})
is precisely
{\mathcal{O}}_{v^{*}_{L}}
– a circular ordering obtained from
{\mathcal{J}}^{*}_{L}
. We discard, for every edge
e\in E^{*}_{L}
, the part of the image of
e
that lies inside disc
\eta(v^{*}_{L})
. Next, we place a smaller disc
\eta^{\prime}(v^{*}_{L})
inside
\eta(v^{*}_{L})
, such that the boundaries of the two discs are disjoint, and the image of
v^{*}_{L}
lies in the interior of
\eta(v^{*}_{L})
. We place images of vertices
v_{L},u_{1}^{\prime},u_{2}^{\prime},\ldots,u_{|Z_{1}|}^{\prime}
on the boundary of
\eta(v^{*}_{L})
in this order. We then extend the images of the edges
e\in E^{*}_{L}
inside
\eta(v^{*}_{L})\setminus\eta^{\prime}(v^{*}_{L})
, so that the image of each edge now terminates at the image of its endpoint. From the definition of the ordering
{\mathcal{J}}^{*}_{L}
, we can do so without introducing any new crossings.
We process the drawing
\varphi_{G_{2}}
of graph
G_{2}
in exactly the same way.

Consider now the sphere, and a simple closed curve
\sigma
on the sphere, that splits it into two discs,
D
and
D^{\prime}
. We place the drawing that we have obtained from
\varphi_{G_{1}}
inside disc
D
, so that the boundary of
\eta^{\prime}(v^{*}_{L})
coincides with the boundary of
D
. We also place the drawing that we have obtained from
\varphi_{G_{2}}
inside disc
D^{\prime}
, so that the boundary of
\eta^{\prime}(v^{*}_{R})
coincides with the boundary of
D^{\prime}
, and, moreover, for all
1\leq i\leq|Z_{1}^{\prime}|
, the images of vertices
u_{i}^{\prime},u_{i}^{\prime\prime}
coincide, and the images of vertices
v_{L},v_{R}
coincide as well (vertex
v_{L}
represents the merger of vertices
v^{\prime}_{R},v^{\prime\prime}_{R}
in
G_{R}
). So far we have not introduced any new crossings. It now remains to add the images of the edges in
E^{\prime}
to the resulting drawing. We do so using Lemma
9.2
, obtaining a drawing
\varphi_{G^{\prime}}
of
G^{\prime}
, with the number of crossings bounded by:

\mathsf{cr}(\varphi_{G_{1}})+\mathsf{cr}(\varphi_{G_{2}})+|E^{\prime}|\cdot|E(G^{\prime})|.

Assuming that
{\cal{E}}
did not happen, then
|E^{\prime}|\leq 2\chi^{1-4\epsilon}
, and so the number of crossings is bounded by:

\mathsf{cr}(\varphi_{G_{1}})+\mathsf{cr}(\varphi_{G_{2}})+2\chi^{1-4\epsilon}\cdot|E(G^{\prime})|.

9.5
Final Accounting

Let
\varphi_{G}
be a solution to the instance
(G,\Sigma)
of
MCNwRS
that our algorithm computes. We now bound the number of crossings in
\varphi_{G}
. We start by summarizing the properties of our recursive algorithm.
Let
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
be an instance that was considered by the algorithm. Recall that we have denoted by
N(G^{\prime})
the total number of crossings in which the edges of
G^{\prime}
participate in the optimal solution
\varphi^{*}
to instance
(G,\Sigma)
.

•

If
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
is a base instance, then our algorithm is guaranteed to produce a solution to this instance with the number of crossings bounded by
O\left(\left(N(G^{\prime})+\chi^{1-\epsilon}\cdot|E(G^{\prime})|\right)\operatorname{poly}(\log n)\right)
. We say that instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})

contributes

\Upsilon(G^{\prime})=O\left(\left(N(G^{\prime})+\chi^{1-\epsilon}\cdot|E(G^{\prime})|\right)\operatorname{poly}(\log n)\right)
crossings.

•

If Case 1 happens when processing instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
, then
our algorithm decomposes instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
into two subinstances
(G_{1},\Sigma_{1})
and
(G_{2},\Sigma_{2})
, such that
|E(G_{1})|,|E(G_{2})|\geq\chi^{1-\epsilon}/3
(from Observation
9.4
). Our algorithm then computes a solution
\varphi_{G^{\prime}}
to instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
, whose number of crossings is bounded by
\mathsf{cr}(\varphi_{G_{1}})+\mathsf{cr}(\varphi_{G_{2}})+\chi^{1-2\epsilon}\cdot|E(G^{\prime})|
. We say that instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
contributes

\Upsilon(G^{\prime})=\chi^{1-2\epsilon}\cdot|E(G^{\prime})|
new crossings.

•

Lastly, if Case 2 happens when processing instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
, then
our algorithm decomposes instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
into two sub-instances
(G_{1},\Sigma_{1})
and
(G_{2},\Sigma_{2})
. Assuming that bad event
{\cal{E}}
does not happen when processing
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
, we are guaranteed that
|E(G_{1})|,|E(G_{2})|\geq\chi^{1-2\epsilon}/4
. Moreover, our algorithm computes a solution
\varphi_{G^{\prime}}
to instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
, whose number of crossings is bounded by
\mathsf{cr}(\varphi_{G_{1}})+\mathsf{cr}(\varphi_{G_{2}})+2\chi^{1-4\epsilon}\cdot|E(G^{\prime})|
. We say that instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
contributes

\Upsilon(G^{\prime})=2\chi^{1-4\epsilon}\cdot|E(G^{\prime})|
new crossings.

We can associate a
decomposition tree

T
with the execution of the algorithm (see Figure
14
). For every instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
considered by the algorithm, the decomposition tree contains a vertex
v(G^{\prime})
. If instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
was decomposed into subinstances
(G_{1},\Sigma_{1})
and
(G_{2},\Sigma_{2})
, then we add edges
(v(G^{\prime}),v(G_{1}))
and
(v(G^{\prime}),v(G_{2}))
to the tree. The tree is rooted at vertex
v(G)
, and its leaves correspond to the base instances. We denote by
{\mathcal{H}}
the set of all graphs
G^{\prime}
, such that instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
was considered by the algorithm, and we let
{\mathcal{L}}\subseteq{\mathcal{H}}
be the set of graphs
G^{\prime}
whose corresponding vertex
v(G^{\prime})
is a leaf of the tree
T
.

Figure 14:
Decomposition tree associated with the algorithm. Vertices corresponding to base instances are shown in red.

Recall that, whenever instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
is decomposed into sub-instances
(G_{1},\Sigma_{1})
and
(G_{2},\Sigma_{2})
, we have ensured that
E(G_{1}),E(G_{2})\subseteq E(G^{\prime})
and
E(G_{1})\cap E(G_{2})=\emptyset
.
Therefore, for all graphs
G^{\prime},G^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathcal{L}}
,
E(G^{\prime})\cap E(G^{\prime\prime})=\emptyset
.
Since, from Invariant
I1
, for every instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
,
|E(G^{\prime})|\geq\chi^{1-2\epsilon}/4
, and since
|E(G)|\leq\chi
, the number of the recursive levels is bounded by
4\chi^{2\epsilon}
, and
|{\mathcal{L}}|\leq 4\chi^{2\epsilon}
. Moreover, since every inner vertex of tree
T
has
2
children, the total number of vertices in tree
T
is at most
8\chi^{2\epsilon}
. Therefore, we have proved the following claim:

Claim 9.17
.

The number of the recursive levels is at most
4\chi^{2\epsilon}
, and the total number of instances that the algorithm considers is at most
8\chi^{2\epsilon}
.

Recall that we have defined, for every graph
G^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{H}}
, the number of crossings
\Upsilon(G^{\prime})
that
G^{\prime}
contributes. Then the total number of crossings in
\varphi_{G}
is bounded by
\sum_{G^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{H}}}\Upsilon(G^{\prime})
. Let
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\subseteq{\mathcal{H}}
be the set of all graphs
G^{\prime}
, such that Case 1 happened when instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
was considered, and define
{\mathcal{H}}_{2}
similarly for Case 2. We denote
\Upsilon_{0}=\sum_{G^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{L}}}\Upsilon(G^{\prime})
,
\Upsilon_{1}=\sum_{G^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}\Upsilon(G^{\prime})
, and
\Upsilon_{2}=\sum_{G^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{2}}\Upsilon(G^{\prime})
. We now bound each of the three terms separately.

Claim 9.18
.

\Upsilon_{0}\leq O\left(\chi^{2-\epsilon}\operatorname{poly}(\log n)\right).

Proof.

Recall that for each graph
G^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{L}}
,
\Upsilon(G^{\prime})=O\left(\left(N(G^{\prime})+\chi^{1-\epsilon}\cdot|E(G^{\prime})|\right)\operatorname{poly}\log n\right)
. Since the graphs in
{\mathcal{L}}
are disjoint in their edges, and since
\mathsf{cr}(\varphi^{*}),|E(G)|\leq\chi
, we get that:

\begin{split}\Upsilon_{0}&=\sum_{G^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{L}}}\Upsilon(G^{\prime})\\
&\leq\sum_{G^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{L}}}O\left(\left(N(G^{\prime})+\chi^{1-\epsilon}\cdot|E(G^{\prime})|\right)\operatorname{poly}\log n\right)\\
&\leq O\left(\left(\mathsf{cr}(\varphi^{*})+\chi^{1-\epsilon}\cdot|E(G)|\right)\operatorname{poly}\log n\right)\\
&\leq O\left(\chi^{2-\epsilon}\operatorname{poly}\log n\right).\end{split}

Claim 9.19
.

With probability at least
1/2
,
\Upsilon_{2}\leq O(\chi^{2-\epsilon})
.

Proof.

For a graph
G^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{2}
, let
{\cal{E}}(G^{\prime})
denote the bad event
{\cal{E}}
for instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
. Recall that
{\cal{E}}(G^{\prime})
happens with probability at most
O(1/\chi^{3\epsilon})
. Since, from Claim
9.17
,
|{\mathcal{H}}_{2}|\leq 8\chi^{2\epsilon}
, from the union bound, with probability at least
1/2
, none of the events in
\left\{{\cal{E}}(G^{\prime})\mid G^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{2}\right\}
happens.

For the remainder of the proof, we assume that none of the above events happens. Recall that for each graph
G^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{2}
,
\Upsilon(G^{\prime})=2\chi^{1-4\epsilon}\cdot|E(G^{\prime})|
. We partition the graphs in
{\mathcal{H}}_{2}
into
levels
, where two graphs
G_{1},G_{2}
lie in the same level iff the distances from
v(G_{1})
and from
v(G_{2})
to the root
v(G)
of the tree
T
are the same. Then the number of levels is bounded by
O(\chi^{2\epsilon})
(from Claim
9.17
), and the total contribution of graphs of
{\mathcal{H}}_{2}
that lie in the same level is bounded by
O(\chi^{1-4\epsilon}\cdot|E(G)|)\leq O(\chi^{2-4\epsilon})
. Therefore, overall,
\Upsilon_{2}\leq O(\chi^{2-\epsilon})
, with probability at least
1/2
.

Claim 9.20
.

\Upsilon_{1}\leq O(\chi^{2-\epsilon})
.

Proof.

Recall that for each graph
G^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{2}
,
\Upsilon(G^{\prime})=\chi^{1-2\epsilon}\cdot|E(G^{\prime})|
. When instance
(G^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
is processed, we decomposed it into two sub-instances
(G_{1},\Sigma_{1})
and
(G_{2},\Sigma_{2})
, such that
|E(G_{1})|,|E(G_{2})|\geq\chi^{1-\epsilon}/3
.
We mark, in the tree
T
, every vertex
v(G^{\prime})
with
G^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{2}
. We say that instance
G^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{2}

lies at level
i
iff there are exactly
i
marked vertices on the unique path in tree
T
from
v(G^{\prime})
to
v(G)
.

Observation 9.21
.

The total number of non-empty levels is at most
3\chi^{\epsilon}
.

Proof.

Consider any graph
G^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{2}
, and assume that it lies at level
i
. Let
G=G_{1},G_{2},\ldots,G_{i}=G^{\prime}
be the marked vertices on the unique path
P
connecting
v(G^{\prime})
to
v(G)
in
T
. Notice that, for every vertex
v(G_{j})
on path
P
, there is a child
v(G^{\prime}_{j})
of
v(G_{j})
that does not lie on
P
, such that
|E(G_{j}^{\prime})|\geq\chi^{1-\epsilon}/3
. Since the edge sets of all graphs
G_{1}^{\prime},G_{2}^{\prime},\ldots,G_{i}^{\prime}
are mutually disjoint, and since
|E(G)|\leq\chi
, we get that
i\leq 3\chi^{\epsilon}
.

For every
1\leq i\leq 3\chi^{\epsilon}
, the graphs
G^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{2}
that lie at level
i
contribute in total at most
\chi^{1-2\epsilon}\cdot|E(G)|\leq\chi^{2-2\epsilon}
to
\Upsilon_{1}
. Since the number of non-empty levels is bounded by
O(\chi^{\epsilon})
,
\Upsilon_{1}\leq O(\chi^{2-\epsilon})
.

We conclude that, with probability at least
1/2
, the number of crossings in the drawing
\varphi(G)
is bounded by
O(\chi^{2-\epsilon}\cdot\operatorname{poly}\log n)
. In order to increase the probability of success, we repeat the algorithm multiple time, returning the best of the resulting solutions.

Appendix A
Lower Bounds for the Relationship between
Minimum Crossing Number
and
Minimum Planarization

Recall that
[
CMS11
]
provided an efficient algorithm, that, given an input graph
G
, and a planarizing set
E^{\prime}
of
k
edges for
G
, draws the graph with
O\left(\Delta^{3}\cdot k\cdot(\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(G)+k)\right)
crossings, using Paradigm
\Pi
. In this section we provide two lower-bound constructions of
[
CMM16
]
, that show that the algorithm of
[
CMS11
]
provides nearly best possible guarantees that can be achieved via this paradigm.
Specifically, in Figure
15
, we show a graph
G
and a planarizing set
E^{\prime}
of
k
edges of
G
, such that graph
G\setminus E^{\prime}
is
3
-connected, and hence has a unique planar drawing. However, it is easy to see that, adding the images of the edges of
E^{\prime}
to this drawing must incur
\Omega(k\cdot\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(G))
crossings. In Figure
16
, we show another example, due to
[
CMM16
]
, where
\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(G)
may be arbitrarily small relatively to
k
, in which adding the images of edges of
E^{\prime}
to the unique planar drawing of
G\setminus E^{\prime}
must incur
\Omega(k^{2})
crossings.

(a)

Graph
G
and its optimal drawing; the edges of
E^{\prime}
are shown in red.

(b)

The unique planar drawing of graph
G\setminus E^{\prime}
.

(c)

Adding images of edges of
E^{\prime}
to the planar drawing of
G\setminus E^{\prime}
must cause
\Omega(k\cdot\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(G))
crossings.

Figure 15:
A negative example due to
[
CMM16
]
, showing a lower bound of
\Omega(k\cdot\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(G))
on the number of crossings that any algorithm using Paradigm
\Pi
must incur.

(a)

Graph
G
and its optimal drawing; the edges of
E^{\prime}
are shown in red.

(b)

The unique planar drawing of graph
G\setminus E^{\prime}
.

(c)

Adding images of edges in
E^{\prime}
to the planar drawing of
G\setminus E^{\prime}
must cause
\Omega(k^{2})
crossings.

Figure 16:
This example establishes the lower bound of
\Omega(k^{2})
, in the case where
k\geq\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(G)
. We note that this construction can be modified to work for arbitrary values of
k
, while keeping
\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(G)=O(1)
.

Appendix B
Proof of Corollary
1.6

Recall that the algorithm of
[
EGS02
]
computes a drawing of the input graph
G
with

O((n+\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(G))\cdot\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n))
crossings.
In order to obtain an
O(n^{1/2-\epsilon^{\prime}}\cdot\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n))
-approximation algorithm
for
Minimum Crossing Number
, we apply the algorithm from Corollary
1.5
, and the algorithm of
[
EGS02
]
to the input graph
G
, and output the better of the two solutions.
If
\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(G)\geq n^{1/2+\epsilon/4}
, where
\epsilon
is the constant from Corollary
1.5
, then the algorithm of
[
EGS02
]
provides an
O(n^{1/2-\epsilon/4}\cdot\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n))
-approximation. Otherwise,
\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(G)<n^{1/2+\epsilon/4}
, and the algorithm from
Corollary
1.5
achieves an approximation factor of
O\left((\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(G))^{1-\epsilon}\cdot\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n)\right)\leq O\left(n^{1/2-\epsilon/4}\cdot\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n)\right)
. We note that we did not attempt to optimize the constant
\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon/4
.

Appendix C
Proofs Omitted from Section
3

C.1
Proof of Theorem
3.6

In order to simplify the notation, we denote the set
\hat{\mathcal{C}}
of clusters by
{\mathcal{C}}
.
Consider some cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
.
For every face
F\in{\mathcal{F}}_{C}
of the drawing
\psi_{C}
of
C
, we let
{\mathcal{H}}_{C}(F)\subseteq{\mathcal{C}}\setminus\left\{C\right\}
contain all clusters
C^{\prime}
such that
F_{C}(C^{\prime})=F
. We denote by
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}_{C}\subseteq{\mathcal{F}}_{C}
the set of all faces
F
with
{\mathcal{H}}_{C}(F)\neq\emptyset
.

The proof of the theorem a recursive algorithm. The base case is when, for every cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
,
|{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}_{C}|=1
. For each cluster
C
, let
F_{C}
be the unique face in
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}_{C}
. Consider a drawing of the clusters in
\bigcup_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}}C
in the plane, such that the drawing of each cluster
C
is identical to
\psi_{C}
, with the face
F_{C}
serving as the outer face of the drawing of
C
, and the images of all clusters are disjoint. Let
\varphi^{\prime}
denote the resulting drawing of
\bigcup_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}}C
in the plane, and let
\varphi
be the corresponding drawing on the sphere. Then it is easy to see that
\varphi^{\prime}
defines a feasible solution for the input instance of the
Cluster Placement
problem.

Assume now that there is at least one cluster
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
, with
|{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}(C)|>1
. For every face
F\in{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}(C)
, we define a new sub-instance of the current instance of the
Cluster Placement
problem, that only includes the clusters of
{\mathcal{H}}_{C}(F)\cup\left\{C\right\}
; the faces
F_{C_{1}}(C_{2})
for pairs
C_{1},C_{2}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{C}(F)
of clusters are defined exactly as before. Notice that, if the original instance of the
Cluster Placement
problem had a feasible solution, then each resulting sub-problem must also have a feasible solution. We solve each such sub-problem recursively, and obtain, for every face
F\in{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}(C)
, a drawing
\varphi_{F}
of
\bigcup_{C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{C}(F)\cup C}C^{\prime}
on the sphere. We let
D_{F}
denote a disc in this drawing that contains the images of all clusters if
{\mathcal{H}}_{C}(F)
but is disjoint form the image of
C
. In order to obtain a solution to the original instance of the problem, we start with the drawing
\psi_{C}
of the cluster
C
on the sphere. For every face
F\in{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}(C)
, we copy the contents of the disc
D_{F}
in the drawing
\varphi_{F}
to the interior of the face
F
. Once we process every face
F\in{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}(C)
, we obtain a drawing of
\bigcup_{C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}}C^{\prime}
. Moreover, it is easy to verify that, if the original instance of the
Cluster Placement
problem had a feasible solution, then we obtain a feasible solution for this instance.

C.2
Proof of Lemma
3.9

It is enough to show that, for every face
F\in{\mathcal{F}}
, there is a solution
\varphi^{F}
to instance
(G^{F},\Sigma^{F})
of
MCNwRS
, such that, if we denote by
\chi^{F}
the set of all crossings of edges in
\varphi^{F}
, then
\sum_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}|\chi^{F}|\leq O(\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(G)\cdot\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n))
.

We now fix a face
F\in{\mathcal{F}}
and construct a solution
\varphi^{F}
to instance
(G^{F},\Sigma^{F})
of
MCNwRS
. Our starting point is the drawing
\varphi
of graph
G^{\prime}
given by Observation
3.7
. We delete from
\varphi
the images of all vertices and edges, except for the vertices and edges of the clusters in
{\mathcal{H}}(F)
, and the images of the edges in
E^{F}
. Recall that, in the resulting drawing, no edge of
\bigcup_{C\in{\mathcal{H}}(F)}E(C)
may participate in crossings. Since, for every ordered pair
(C,C^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{H}}(C)
of clusters, the image of
C^{\prime}
in the resulting drawing must be contained in the face
F_{C}(C^{\prime})
, there must be a face
F^{\prime}
in the resulting drawing that contains the images of every edge in
E^{F}
. Viewing face
F^{\prime}
as the outer face of a planar drawing of the resulting graph, we can contract the image of each cluster
C\in{\mathcal{H}}(F)
into a single point, that we view as the image of the corresponding vertex
v(C)
, without increasing the number of crossings. Therefore, we obtain a drawing
\tilde{\varphi}^{F}
of the graph
G^{F}
on the sphere, and moreover:

\sum_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}\mathsf{cr}(\tilde{\varphi}^{F})\leq\mathsf{cr}(\varphi)\leq O(\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(G)\cdot\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n)).

Consider again some face
F\in{\mathcal{F}}
. Notice that the drawing
\tilde{\varphi}^{F}
of
G^{F}
is not necessarily consistent with the rotation system
\Sigma^{F}
. Next, we modify the drawing
\tilde{\varphi}^{F}
in order to make it consistent with
\Sigma^{F}
, while only introducing a small number of crossings. Recall that we have defined a set
\Gamma\subseteq V(G^{\prime})
of vertices called terminals – the set of all vertices that serve as endpoints of the edges in
E^{\prime\prime}
.

We process the vertices
v\in V(G^{F})
one-by-one. For every vertex
v\in V(G^{F})
, we let
\eta(v)
be a small disc around the drawing of
v
in
\tilde{\varphi}^{F}
. When vertex
v
is processed, we slightly modify the images of the edges of
\delta(v)
in disc
\eta(v)
, so that the circular order in which the edges of
\delta(v)
enter
v
becomes identical to
{\mathcal{O}}_{v}
.

Consider now some vertex
v=v(C)\in V(G^{F})
. Assume first that
C\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
. In this case,
|\delta(v)|\leq\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n)
. For every edge
e\in\delta(v)
, we replace the segment of the drawing of
e
in disc
\eta(v)
, so that all resulting curves enter the image of the vertex
v
in the order
{\mathcal{O}}_{v}
, and every pair of curves intersects at most once. This introduces at most
\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n)
new crossings.

Assume now that
C\in{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
. Let
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}_{v}
be the order in which the images of edges of
\delta(v)
enter
v
in the current drawing
\tilde{\varphi}^{F}
. Recall that we have defined a cycle
K^{F}(C)\subseteq C
, which is the intersection of the cluster
C
and the boundary of the face
F
, and
\Gamma^{F}(C)\subseteq\Gamma(C)
is the set of terminals that appear on
K^{F}(C)
. Recall that we have defined an ordering
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}^{F}(C)
of the terminals in
\Gamma^{F}(C)
to be the circular order of the terminals of
\Gamma^{F}(C)
along the cycle
K^{F}(C)
, and the ordering
{\mathcal{O}}_{v}
of
\delta(v)
was defined based on
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}^{F}(C)
. Since the edges of
C
may not participate in crossings in
\varphi
, and since the drawing of
C
in
\varphi
is identical to
\psi_{C}
, for every terminal
t\in\Gamma^{F}(C)
, the edges of
\delta(v)
that are incident to
t
appear consecutively in the ordering
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}_{v}
(and from the definition, they also appear consecutively in the ordering
{\mathcal{O}}_{v}
). The orderings of edges of
\delta(v)
that are incident to different terminals in
{\mathcal{O}}_{v}
and
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}_{v}
must both be consistent with
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}_{v}
. Therefore, the only difference between the orderings
{\mathcal{O}}_{v}
and
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}_{v}
is that for every terminal
t\in\Gamma^{F}(C)
, the edges of
\delta(v)
that are incident to
t
may appear in different orders in
{\mathcal{O}}_{v}
and
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}_{v}
. Consider the small disc
\eta(v)
around the vertex
v
in the current drawing
\tilde{\varphi}^{F}
of
G^{F}
. We can assume that this disc is small enough so it does not contain any crossings. For every edge
e\in\delta(v)
, let
p_{e}
be the point that is the intersection of the current image of
e
and the boundary of the disc
\eta(v)
in
\tilde{\varphi}^{F}
. Then for every terminal
t\in\Gamma^{F}(C)
, the points
p_{e}
corresponding to the edges of
\delta(v)
that are incident to
t
appear consecutively on the boundary of
\eta(v)
. We rearrange the images of all edges of
\delta(v)
that are incident to
t
inside
\eta(v)
, so that they enter
v
in the order consistent with
{\mathcal{O}}_{v}
. This introduces, for every terminal
t\in\Gamma^{F}(C)
, at most
O(\Delta^{2})
new crossings. Once we process all vertices of
G^{F}
, we obtain the final drawing
\varphi^{F}
of
G^{F}
that is consistent with the rotation system
\Sigma^{F}
. We now bound the total number of new crossings that this procedure has introduced.

Consider a cluster
C
. Note that for every terminal
t\in\Gamma(C)
, there can be at most
\Delta
faces
F\in{\mathcal{F}}
, such that
t
lies on the boundary of
F
in drawing
\tilde{\varphi}
of
\bigcup_{C\in{\mathcal{C}}}C
. Therefore, there are at most
\Delta|\Gamma(C)|
pairs
(t,F)
, where
t\in\Gamma(C)
and
F\in{\mathcal{F}}
, such that
t
lies on the boundary of
F
. We denote the set of all such pairs for cluster
C
by
\Pi(C)
.

If
C
is a type-1 cluster, then the total increase in the number of crossings due to rearranging edges entering vertex
v(C)
in all graphs
\left\{G^{F}\right\}_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}
is at most
O(|\Pi(C)|^{2}\Delta^{2})\leq O(\Delta^{4}|\Gamma(C)|^{2})\leq O(\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n))
.

If
C
is a type-2 cluster, then every pair
(t,F)\in\Pi(C)
contributes at most
\Delta^{2}
crossings (by rearranging the images of edges of
\delta(v)
in
\tilde{\varphi}^{F}
that are incident to
t
).

Altogether, the number of new crossings is bounded by:

\begin{split}O(|\Gamma|\Delta^{3})+O(|{\mathcal{C}}_{1}|\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n))&\leq O(|\Gamma|\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n))\\
&\leq O(|E^{\prime\prime}|\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n))\\
&\leq O(\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(G)\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n),\end{split}

and so the total number of crossings in all drawings in
\left\{\varphi^{F}\right\}_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}
is bounded by
O(\mathsf{OPT}_{\mathsf{cr}}(G)\operatorname{poly}(\Delta\log n))
.

Appendix D
Proofs Omitted from Section
4

D.1
Proof of Lemma
4.4

We assume w.l.o.g. that graph
G
is
2
-connected, as otherwise we can prove the theorem for each of its super-blocks
Z\in{\mathcal{Z}}(G)
separately. We denote by
{\mathcal{B}}={\mathcal{B}}(G)
the block decomposition of
G
,
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}=\left\{\tilde{B}\mid B\in{\mathcal{B}}\right\}
, and we let
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{*}\subseteq\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
contain all graphs
\tilde{B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
that are not isomorphic to
K_{3}
. We denote by
\tau=\tau({\mathcal{B}})
the decomposition tree corresponding to the block decomposition
{\mathcal{B}}
of
G
. If vertex
v(B_{1})
is a child of vertex
v(B_{2})
in tree
\tau
, then we say that
B_{1}
is a
child block
of
B_{2}
. For a block
B\in{\mathcal{B}}
, we also denote by
\operatorname{Desc}(B)
the set of all
descendant blocks
of
B
. The set
\operatorname{Desc}(B)
contains all blocks
B_{1}
where vertex
v(B_{1})
is a descendant of vertex
v(B)
in the tree
\tau
. We note that the set
\operatorname{Desc}(B)
also contains the block
B
.

Consider a block
B\in{\mathcal{B}}
. For convenience, we call the fake edge
e^{*}_{\tilde{B}}
connecting the endpoints of
B
(if it exists) a
bad
fake edge, and all other fake edges of
\tilde{B}
are called good fake edges. We define a set
A^{\prime}_{\tilde{B}}
of fake edges as follows: if
B
is isomorphic to
K_{3}
, then
A^{\prime}_{\tilde{B}}=\emptyset
, and otherwise,
A^{\prime}_{\tilde{B}}
contains all good fake edges of
\tilde{B}
. Consider now some good fake edge
e=(u,v)\in A^{\prime}_{\tilde{B}}
. Then there is some child block
B_{1}
of
B
with endpoints
u,v
. A
valid embedding
of the fake edge
e=(u,v)\in A^{\prime}_{\tilde{B}}
is a path
P(e)
that connects
u
to
v
, is internally disjoint from
\tilde{B}
, and is contained in a block
B_{1}
that is a child block of
B
, whose endpoints are
(u,v)
. A
valid embedding
of the set
A^{\prime}_{\tilde{B}}
of fake edges is a collection
{\mathcal{Q}}(B)=\left\{P(e)\mid e\in A^{\prime}_{\tilde{B}}\right\}
of paths, were for each edge
e\in A^{\prime}_{\tilde{B}}
, path
P(e)
is a valid embedding of
e
. Note that from the definition, we are guaranteed that the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}(B)
are internally disjoint. The following lemma is central to the proof of Lemma
4.4
.

Lemma D.1
.

There is an efficient algorithm, that, given a block
B\in{\mathcal{B}}\setminus\left\{G\right\}
, computes, for every descendant-block
B_{1}\in\operatorname{Desc}(B)
, a valid embedding
{\mathcal{Q}}(B_{1})
of the set
A^{\prime}_{\tilde{B}_{1}}
of fake edges, and additionally a collection
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}(B)
of
6
paths in
B
, connecting the endpoints of
B
, such that, if we denote by
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(B)=\bigcup_{B_{1}\in\operatorname{Desc}(B)}{\mathcal{Q}}(B_{1})
, then the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}(B)\cup{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(B)
cause congestion at most
6
in
B
.

We note that, from the definition of valid embeddings, all paths in
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(B)
must be contained in
B
.

We prove Lemma
D.1
below, after we complete the proof of Lemma
4.4
using it.
Recall that graph
G
itself is a block in the decomposition
{\mathcal{B}}
. Let
B_{1},\ldots,B_{r}
be the child blocks of
G
. We apply the algorithm from
Lemma
D.1
to each such block
B_{i}
separately, obtaining the sets
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}(B_{i}),{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(B_{i})
of paths.
Let
{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{r}{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(B_{i})
. Then set
{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
contains, for every block
B\in{\mathcal{B}}\setminus\left\{G\right\}
, a set
{\mathcal{Q}}(B)
of paths that defines a valid embedding of the set
A^{\prime}_{\tilde{B}}
of fake edges of
\tilde{B}
.
For all
1\leq i\leq r
, let
(x_{i},y_{i})
be the endpoints of the block
B_{i}
. We embed the fake edge
(x_{i},y_{i})
of
A^{\prime}_{\tilde{G}}
into any of the
6
paths
P(x_{i},y_{i})\in{\mathcal{P}}_{1}(B_{i})
. We then set
{\mathcal{Q}}(G)=\left\{P(x_{i},y_{i})\mid 1\leq i\leq r\right\}
. Note that
{\mathcal{Q}}(G)
is a valid embedding of the good fake edges of
G
. Adding the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}(G)
to the set
{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
, we obtain a set of paths in
G
, that cause edge-congestion at most
6
, and contain, for every pseudo-block
B\in{\mathcal{B}}
, a valid embedding of the set
A^{\prime}_{\tilde{B}}
of fake edges. Lastly, consider any block
B\in{\mathcal{B}}\setminus\left\{G\right\}
. Let
(x,y)
be the endpoints of
B
, and let
B^{c}
be its complement block. Then
B^{c}
contains a path connecting
x
to
y
. We let the embedding
P(e^{*}_{\tilde{B}})
of the bad fake edge
e^{*}_{\tilde{B}}
be any path in
B^{c}
that connects
x
to
y
.
We then set
{\mathcal{P}}_{\tilde{B}}={\mathcal{Q}}(B)\cup\left\{P(e^{*}_{\tilde{B}})\right\}
.
Observe that, from the definition of valid embeddings of bad fake edges, all paths in
{\mathcal{P}}_{\tilde{B}}
are mutually internally disjoint, and they are internally disjoint from
\tilde{B}
. As discussed above, the paths in set
{\mathcal{P}}=\bigcup_{\tilde{B}\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{*}(G)}\left({\mathcal{P}}_{\tilde{B}}\setminus\left\{P_{\tilde{B}}(e^{*}_{\tilde{B}})\right\}\right)
cause congestion at most
6
in
G
. Therefore, in order to complete the proof of Lemma
4.4
, it is now enough to prove Lemma
D.1
.

Proof of Lemma
D.1
.

The proof is by induction on the length of the longest path from
v(B)
to a leaf vertex of
\tau
that is a descendant of
v(B)
in
\tau
.
The base case is when
v(B)
is a leaf vertex of
\tau
. Let
(x,y)
denote the endpoints of
B
. Observe that in this case,
\tilde{B}
is obtained from block
B
by adding the bad fake edge
e^{*}_{\tilde{B}}
to it, and this is the only fake edge in
\tilde{B}
. In particular,
A^{\prime}_{\tilde{B}}=\emptyset
, so we can set
{\mathcal{Q}}(B)=\emptyset
. Block
B
must contain at least one path
P
connecting
x
to
y
. We let
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}(B)
contain
6
copies of this path. Setting
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(B)=\emptyset
, we get valid sets
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}(B),{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(B)
of paths for
B
, with
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}(B)\cup{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(B)
causing edge-congestion at most
6
in
B
.

Consider now an arbitrary block
B\in{\mathcal{B}}\setminus\left\{G\right\}
, such that
v(B)
is not a leaf of
\tau
, and let
B_{1},\ldots,B_{r}
be its child blocks. Using the induction hypothesis, we compute, for all
1\leq i\leq r
, the sets
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}(B_{i}),{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(B_{i})
of paths that are contained in
B_{i}
, such that
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}(B_{i})\cup{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(B_{i})
cause edge-congestion at most
6
in
B_{i}
. We now consider three cases.

Case 1.
\tilde{B}=K_{3}
.

In this case,
A^{\prime}_{\tilde{B}}=\emptyset
, and so
{\mathcal{Q}}(B)=\emptyset
is a valid embedding of the edges in
A^{\prime}_{\tilde{B}}
. We set
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(B)=\bigcup_{i=1}^{r}{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(B_{i})
. Clearly, set
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(B)
contains, for each block
B^{*}\in\operatorname{Desc}(B)
, a valid embedding
{\mathcal{Q}}(B^{*})
of the edges of
A^{\prime}_{\tilde{B}^{*}}
. It now remains to define a set
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}(B)
of
6
paths connecting the endpoints of
B
. Let
(x,y)
denote the endpoints of
B
. Then there is a path
P
in
\tilde{B}
, that is disjoint from the bad fake edge
e^{*}_{\tilde{B}}
, connecting
x
to
y
. This path contains two edges, that we denote by
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
. Initially, we let
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}(B)
contain six copies of the path
P
, that we denote by
P_{1},\ldots,P_{6}
. Assume first that
e_{1}=(x_{1},y_{1})
is a fake edge, and assume w.l.o.g. that the child block
B_{1}
of
B
has endpoints
x_{1}
and
y_{1}
. Then we replace, for all
1\leq i\leq 6
, the edge
e_{1}
on path
P_{i}
by the
i
th path in set
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}(B_{1})
(recall that this path connects
x_{1}
to
y_{1}
in
B_{1}
). If
e_{2}
is a fake edge, then we proceed similarly. As a result, set
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}(B)
now contains
6
paths that are contained in
B
, each of which connects
x
to
y
. Moreover, it is easy to verify that the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}(B)\cup{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(B)
cause edge-congestion at most
6
in
B
.

Case 2.
\tilde{B}\neq K_{3}
, and block
B
has a single child-block.

We denote by
B_{1}
the child block of
B
. Let
(x,y)
be the endpoints of
B
, and let
(x_{1},y_{1})
be the endpoints of
B_{1}
. Let
e_{1}=(x_{1},y_{1})
be the unique fake edge in
A^{\prime}_{\tilde{B}}
. We let the embedding of this edge
P(e_{1})
be any of the
6
paths in
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}(B_{1})
. We then let
{\mathcal{Q}}(B)=\left\{P(e_{1})\right\}
be a valid embedding of the edges of
A^{\prime}_{\tilde{B}}
, and
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(B)={\mathcal{Q}}(B)\cup{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(B_{1})
. Clearly, set
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(B)
contains, for each block
B^{*}\in\operatorname{Desc}(B)
, a valid embedding
{\mathcal{Q}}(B^{*})
of the edges of
A^{\prime}_{\tilde{B}^{*}}
. It now remains to define a set
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}(B)
of
6
paths connecting the endpoints of
B
. Since graph
\tilde{B}
is
3
-connected, there are at least three internally disjoint paths connecting
x
to
y
in
\tilde{B}
. Since graph
\tilde{B}
contains only two fake edges, at least one of these paths, that we denote by
P
, does not contain fake edges. We then let
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}(B)
contain
6
copies of the path
P
. Clearly, the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}(B)\cup{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(B)
cause edge-congestion at most
6
in
B
.

Case 3.
\tilde{B}\neq K_{3}
, and block
B
has at least two child-blocks.

Let
(x,y)
be the endpoints of
B
, and, for all
1\leq i\leq r
, let
(x_{i},y_{i})
be the endpoints of the child block
B_{i}
. We also denote by
e_{i}=(x_{i},y_{i})
the fake edge in
A^{\prime}_{\tilde{B}}
connecting the endpoints of
e_{i}
(if it exists). For each block
B_{i}
, we let
P^{*}_{i}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{1}(B_{i})
be an arbitrary path in
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}(B_{i})
, and we let the embedding
P(e_{i})
of fake edge
e_{i}
(if it exists) be
P^{*}_{i}
. We then define
{\mathcal{Q}}(B)=\left\{P(e_{i})\mid e_{i}\in A^{\prime}_{\tilde{B}}\right\}
. It is immediate to verify that
{\mathcal{Q}}(B)
is a valid embedding of the fake edges in
A^{\prime}_{\tilde{B}}
. We then set
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(B)={\mathcal{Q}}(B)\cup\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^{r}{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(B_{i})\right)
, so set
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(B)
contains, for each block
B^{*}\in\operatorname{Desc}(B)
, a valid embedding
{\mathcal{Q}}(B^{*})
of the edges of
A^{\prime}_{\tilde{B}^{*}}
. It now remains to define a set
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}(B)
of
6
paths connecting the endpoints of
B
.

Since graph
\tilde{B}
is
3
-connected, it contains at least three internally disjoint paths connecting
x
to
y
. At least two of these paths, that we denote by
P
and
P^{\prime}
, are disjoint from the bad fake edge
e^{*}_{\tilde{B}}
. We let
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}(B)=\left\{P_{1},P_{2},P_{3},P^{\prime}_{1},P^{\prime}_{2},P^{\prime}_{3}\right\}
, where initially, paths
P_{1},P_{2},P_{3}
are copies of
P
, and paths
P^{\prime}_{1},P^{\prime}_{2},P^{\prime}_{3}
are copies of
P^{\prime}
. Next, we consider every fake edge of
P
and
P^{\prime}
one-by-one. Let
e_{i}\in P
be a fake edge on path
P
, so
e_{i}=(x_{i},y_{i})
, and the child block
B_{i}
of
B
has endpoints
x_{i},y_{i}
. Recall that
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}(B_{i})
contains
6
paths connecting
x_{i}
to
y_{i}
; one of these paths has been used as
P^{*}_{i}
, and the remaining five paths have not been used yet. For each
1\leq j\leq 3
, we replace, on path
P_{j}
, the edge
e_{i}
, with the
j
th path in
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}(B_{i})\setminus\left\{P^{*}_{i}\right\}
. Once we process every fake edge on path
P
and on path
P^{\prime}
in this fashion, we obtain a final set
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}
of
6
paths connecting
x
to
y
in graph
B
. Moreover, it is easy to verify that the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}(B)\cup{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(B)
cause edge-congestion at most
6
.

D.2
Proof of Lemma
4.5

In this section we provide the proof of Lemma
4.5
.

Let
C_{2}\in{\mathcal{C}}(H_{2})
be the connected component of
H_{2}
containing
B_{2}
. Since
H_{2}\subseteq H_{1}
, there is a connected component
C_{1}\in{\mathcal{C}}(H_{1})
containing
C_{2}
. Let
Z_{2}\in{\mathcal{Z}}(C_{2})
be the super-block containing
B_{2}
. Since
C_{2}\subseteq C_{1}
, it is easy to see that there is a super-block
Z_{1}\in{\mathcal{Z}}(C_{1})
containing
Z_{2}
.

We now consider the block decomposition
{\mathcal{L}}(Z_{1})
of
Z_{1}
. We let
B_{1}\in{\mathcal{L}}(Z_{1})
be the pseudo-block containing all vertices of
\tilde{B}_{2}
, that minimizes
|V(B_{1})|
. Since
B_{2}\subseteq Z_{1}
and
Z_{1}\in{\mathcal{L}}(Z_{1})
, such a pseudo-block must exist. We now show that
B_{1}
has all required properties.

We start by showing that
V(\tilde{B}_{2})\subseteq V(\tilde{B}_{1})
. Assume for contradiction that this is not the case. Since
V(\tilde{B}_{2})\subseteq V(B_{1})
, there must be a child block
B_{3}
of
B_{1}
that contains at least one vertex of
\tilde{B}_{2}
as an inner vertex. However,
B_{3}
does not contain all vertices of
\tilde{B}_{2}
, or we would have chosen
B_{3}
instead of
B_{1}
. Therefore, there are two vertices
u,v\in\tilde{B}_{2}
, with
u\in B_{3}
and
v\not\in B_{3}
. The two endpoints of
B_{3}
then disconnect
u
from
v
in graph
H_{1}
. We next show that this is impossible.
Since
|V(\tilde{B}_{2})|>3
and
\tilde{B}_{2}
is
3
-connected, there are three internally disjoint paths in
\tilde{B}_{2}
connecting
u
to
v
, that we denote by
P_{1},P_{2}
and
P_{3}
; note that these paths may contain fake edges. However, from Observation
4.2
, for every fake edge
e
in
\tilde{B}_{2}
, there is a path
P(e)
in
H_{2}
, connecting its endpoints, that is internally disjoint from
V(\tilde{B}_{2})
. Moreover, if
e\neq e^{\prime}
are two distinct fake edges of
\tilde{B}_{2}
, then
P(e)
and
P(e^{\prime})
are internally disjoint. By replacing every fake edge on paths
P_{1},P_{2}
and
P_{3}
with the corresponding path in
H_{2}
, we obtain three internally disjoint paths
P_{1}^{\prime},P_{2}^{\prime},P_{3}^{\prime}
in graph
H_{2}
connecting
u
to
v
. Since
H_{2}\subseteq H_{1}
, these three paths belong to
H_{1}
as well. However, from the definition of blocks, the endpoints of the block
B_{3}
must disconnect
u
from
v
in
H_{1}
, a contradictiton.
We conclude that
V(\tilde{B}_{2})\subseteq V(\tilde{B}_{1})
.

Consider now some real edge
e=(u,v)\in E(\tilde{B}_{2})
. Since
u,v\in\tilde{B}_{1}
, from the definition of graph
\tilde{B}_{1}
, edge
e
must also belong to
\tilde{B}_{1}
, unless
u
and
v
are the endpoints of the block
B_{1}
.

Let
A
be the collection of all fake edges in
\tilde{B}_{2}
. From Observation
4.2
, for every fake edge
e
in
\tilde{B}_{2}
, there is a path
P(e)
in
H_{2}
, connecting its endpoints, that is internally disjoint from
V(\tilde{B}_{2})
. Moreover, the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}=\left\{P(e)\mid e\in A\right\}
are internally disjoint. Since
H_{2}\subseteq H_{1}
, the paths of
{\mathcal{P}}
also belong to graph
H_{1}
. Consider any such path
P=P(e)
. Note that not all edges of
P
may lie in
\tilde{B}_{1}
. We partition the path
P
into segments
\sigma_{1},\eta_{1},\sigma_{2},\eta_{2},\ldots,\eta_{r-1},\sigma_{r}
, where for all
1\leq i\leq r
,
\sigma_{i}
is contained in
\tilde{B}_{1}
(where all edges of
\sigma_{i}
are real edges), and for all
1\leq i<r
,
\eta_{i}
is internally disjoint from
\tilde{B}_{1}
. It is possible that a segment
\sigma_{i}
only consists of a single vertex. We refer to the segments
\sigma_{i}
as
\sigma
-segments
, and we refer to the segments
\eta_{i}
as
\eta
-segments.
We now consider two cases. The first case happens when there is a single
\eta
-segment, and both
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}
contain a single vertex. In this case we say that path
P
is a
type-1
path. Let
u,v
be the endpoints of
P
. Then either
u,v
are the endpoints of
B_{1}
, or there is a child block
B_{3}
of
B_{1}
whose endpoints are
u,v
. In either case,
(u,v)
is a fake edge of
\tilde{B}_{1}
.

Assume now that the first case does not happen; we then say that path
P
is a type-2 path.
Consider now some segment
\eta_{i}
, for
1\leq i<r
, and let
(v_{i},u_{i})
be its endpoints. Then either
(v_{i},u_{i})
are the endpoints of the block
B_{1}
(and then fake edge
(v_{i},u_{i})
lies in
\tilde{B}_{1}
); or
(v_{i},u_{i})
are the endpoints of some child block
B_{3}
of
B_{1}
(in which case the fake edge
(v_{i},u_{i})
also lies in
\tilde{B}_{1}
). We replace each such segment
\eta_{i}
with the corresponding fake edge of
\tilde{B}_{1}
, obtaining a new path
P^{\prime}(e)
that is contained in
\tilde{B}_{1}
. Consider now the resulting set
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}=\left\{P^{\prime}(e)\mid e\in A\right\}
of paths. Recall that the original paths in
\left\{P(e)\mid e\in A\right\}
were internally disjoint. It is easy to see from our construction that the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
are internally disjoint.

Finally, denote the endpoints of
B_{1}
by
u
and
v
, and assume that
\tilde{B}_{2}
contains edge
(u,v)
as a real edge. Recall that, by the definition,
\tilde{B}_{1}
does not contain a real edge
(u,v)
, but it contains a fake edge
e=(u,v)
. We next show that this edge does not lie on any path in
{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
. Assume for contradiction that some path
P^{\prime}(e^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\prime}
